**arXiv:2604.03216v1** (cs.CL)

**Title:** BAS: A Decision-Theoretic Approach to Evaluating Large Language Model Confidence

**Authors:** Sean Wu, Fredrik K. Gustafsson, Edward Phillips, Boyan Gao, Anshul Thakur, David A. Clifton

**Abstract:**
Large language models (LLMs) often provide confident but incorrect answers in scenarios where abstaining would be safer. The paper introduces the Behavioral Alignment Score (BAS), a decision-theoretic metric to evaluate LLM confidence in abstention-aware decision making. BAS is derived from an explicit answer-or-abstain utility model and measures decision-level reliability based on confidence magnitude and ordering. Theoretical analysis shows that truthful confidence estimates maximize expected BAS utility, linking calibration to optimal decision-making behavior. Unlike log loss, BAS imposes an asymmetric penalty prioritizing avoidance of overconfident errors. The study constructs a benchmark of self-reported confidence reliability across multiple LLMs and tasks, revealing significant variation in decision-useful confidence. Larger and more accurate models tend to achieve higher BAS, but even frontier models remain prone to severe overconfidence. Simple interventions like top-$k$ confidence elicitation and post-hoc calibration can improve confidence reliability.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.03216v1
**arXiv:2604.03202v1** (cs.CY)

**Title:** Prosocial Persuasion at Scale? Large Language Models Outperform Humans in Donation Appeals Across Levels of Personalization

**Abstract:** Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly seen as capable of generating persuasive content at scale. This study investigates whether LLM-generated donation appeals are as effective as those written by humans, considering different levels of personalization. Two preregistered online experiments (N = 658 and N = 642) showed that LLM-generated content resulted in more donations, higher engagement, and was rated as more persuasive than human-authored content. Personalization led to increased effectiveness, while false personalization had a negative impact.

**Subjects:** Computers and Society (cs.CY)

**Title:** Understanding the Role of Hallucination in Reinforcement Post-Training of Multimodal Reasoning Models

**Authors:** Gengwei Zhang, Jie Peng, Zhen Tan, Mufan Qiu, Hossein Nourkhiz Mahjoub, Vaishnav Tadiparthi, Kwonjoon Lee, Yanyong Zhang, Tianlong Chen

**Abstract:** This work explores the impact of reinforcement learning (RL) post-training on multimodal reasoning models, focusing on the phenomenon of model hallucination. The authors introduce the Hallucination-as-Cue Framework, which uses modality-specific corruptions to investigate how RL training affects multimodal reasoning models. Through extensive experiments, they find that RL post-training under purely hallucination-inductive settings can significantly improve models' reasoning performance, sometimes outperforming standard training methods. This challenges existing assumptions about multimodal large language model (MLLM) training and highlights the importance of modality-aware RL designs.

**Title:** Detecting and Correcting Reference Hallucinations in Commercial LLMs and Deep Research Agents

**Authors:** Delip Rao, Eric Wong, Chris Callison-Burch

**Abstract:**
Large language models and deep research agents provide citation URLs to support their claims, but the reliability of these citations has not been systematically measured. The study addresses six research questions about citation URL validity using 10 models and agents on DRBench (53,090 URLs) and 3 models on ExpertQA (168,021 URLs across 7 academic fields). It finds that 3-13% of citation URLs are hallucinated and do not exist, while 5-18% are non-resolving overall. Deep research agents generate more citations per query but hallucinate URLs at higher rates. Domain effects are significant, with non-resolving rates ranging from 5.4% (Business) to 11.4% (Theology). The study introduces urlhealth, an open-source tool for URL liveness checking and classification using the Wayback Machine. In self-correction experiments, models equipped with urlhealth reduce non-resolving citation URLs by 6-79 times to under 1%, though effectiveness varies by model competence.

**Subjects:** Computation and Language (cs.CL)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.03173v1

- **Title:** BibTeX Citation Hallucinations in Scientific Publishing Agents: Evaluation and Mitigation
- **Authors:** Delip Rao, Chris Callison-Burch
- **Abstract:** Evaluates large language models with web search in scientific publishing, finding pervasive field-level errors in BibTeX entries. Constructs a benchmark of 931 papers across four domains and three citation tiers to assess model accuracy. Evaluates three search-enabled models (GPT-5, Claude Sonnet-4.6, Gemini-3 Flash) with overall accuracy of 83.6%, dropping to 50.9% for fully correct entries. Identifies failure modes and evaluates clibib tool for mitigation, achieving 91.5% accuracy and 78.3% fully correct entries in a two-stage integration process.

**Title:** Valence-Arousal Subspace in LLMs: Circular Emotion Geometry and Multi-Behavioral Control

**Authors:** Lihao Sun, Lewen Yan, Xiaoya Lu, Andrew Lee, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao

**Abstract:** We present a method to identify a valence-arousal (VA) subspace within large language model representations. From 211k emotion-labeled texts, we derive emotion steering vectors and learn VA axes via ridge regression on the model's self-reported valence-arousal scores. The VA subspace exhibits circular geometry consistent with human emotion perception models. Projections correlate with human-crowdsourced VA ratings across 44k lexical items. Steering along VA axes produces monotonic shifts in affective dimensions of model outputs. Increased arousal decreases refusal and increases sycophancy, and vice versa. Effects are consistent across Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B, and Qwen3-14B, demonstrating cross-architecture generality. Tokens associated with refusal occupy low-arousal, negative-valence regions, modulating their emission probability directly through VA steering.

**Subjects:** Computation and Language (cs.CL); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Computers and Society (cs.CY)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.03147v1
**arXiv:2604.03141v1** (cs.CL)

Title: Beyond Precision: Importance-Aware Recall for Factuality Evaluation in Long-Form LLM Generation

Abstract: Evaluating the factuality of long-form output generated by large language models (LLMs) remains challenging, particularly when responses are open-ended and contain many fine-grained factual statements. Existing evaluation methods primarily focus on precision, but this overlooks an important dimension: recall, whether the generated response covers the relevant facts that should be included. The authors propose a comprehensive factuality evaluation framework that measures both precision and recall, leveraging external knowledge sources to construct reference facts and determine their presence in generated text. They introduce an importance-aware weighting scheme based on relevance and salience. Analysis reveals that current LLMs perform better on precision than on recall, indicating that factual incompleteness is a major limitation in long-form generation.

Authors: Nazanin Jafari, James Allan, Mohit Iyyer
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.03141v1

**Title:** A Systematic Security Evaluation of OpenClaw and Its Variants

**Authors:** Yuhang Wang, Haichang Gao, Zhenxing Niu, Zhaoxiang Liu, Wenjing Zhang, Xiang Wang, Shiguo Lian

**Abstract:** This paper presents a systematic security assessment of six OpenClaw-series agent frameworks (OpenClaw, AutoClaw, QClaw, KimiClaw, MaxClaw, and ArkClaw) under multiple backbone models. The study includes 205 test cases covering representative attack behaviors across the full agent execution lifecycle. Results indicate significant security vulnerabilities in all evaluated agents, with reconnaissance and discovery behaviors being the most common weaknesses. Different frameworks expose distinct high-risk profiles, including credential leakage, lateral movement, privilege escalation, and resource development. The findings highlight the need for lifecycle-wide security governance for intelligent agent frameworks beyond prompt-level safeguards.

**Subjects:** Cryptography and Security (cs.CR); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

Title: PAFT: Preservation Aware Fine-Tuning for Minimal-Edit Program Repair

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are effective for automated program repair but often rewrite more code than necessary. PAFT, a preservation-aware fine-tuning method, derives token-level preservation signals and combines them with full-sequence masking and an edit-difficulty curriculum. PAFT improves pass@1 by up to 65.6% and reduces average edit distance (AED) by up to 32.6% compared to standard supervised fine-tuning. On Defects4J with DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B, PAFT outperforms AdaPatcher, improving pass@1 from 5.9% to 10.1% and reducing median AED from 61.0 to 42.0. PAFT preserves stable context and focuses edits on faulty regions, yielding smaller, more localized patches.

**Title:** Co-Evolution of Policy and Internal Reward for Language Agents

**Authors:** Xinyu Wang, Hanwei Wu, Jingwei Song, Shuyuan Zhang, Jiayi Zhang, Fanqi Kong, Tung Sum Thomas Kwok, Xiao-Wen Chang, Yuyu Luo, Chenglin Wu, Bang Liu

**Abstract:** Large language model (LLM) agents learn through interaction with environments but face challenges with sparse and delayed rewards. The proposed method, Self-Guide, generates an internal reward for agents to guide actions during inference and optimize policy during training. **Title:** Same Feedback, Different Source: How AI vs. Human Feedback Attribution and Credibility Shape Learner Behavior in Computing Education

**Authors:** Caitlin Morris, Pattie Maes

**Abstract:** Investigates how attribution of feedback (AI vs. human) and timing affect learner behavior in computing education. Participants (N=148) completed a creative coding tutorial with feedback from a large language model, attributed to either an AI system or a human teaching assistant. Results show that participants spent more time on task when feedback was attributed to a human (d=0.61, p=.013), while delayed delivery increased output complexity. However, 46% of participants did not believe the human attribution, showing worse outcomes than those receiving transparent AI feedback. The study suggests that credible AI attribution may be a lower-risk approach in educational contexts.

Title: MI-Pruner: Crossmodal Mutual Information-guided Token Pruner for Efficient MLLMs

Title: STEAR: Layer-Aware Spatiotemporal Evidence Intervention for Hallucination Mitigation in Video Large Language Models

**Title:** R2-Write: Reflection and Revision for Open-Ended Writing with Deep Reasoning

**Authors:** Wanlong Liu, Bo Zhang, Chenliang Li, Shaopeng Lai, Yuning Wu, Xuanyu Lei, Ming Yan

**Abstract:** Deep reasoning with long chain-of-thought has improved large language models in verifiable domains like mathematics, but its effectiveness for open-ended writing tasks remains unexplored. This paper reveals that existing reasoning models achieve limited gains on open-ended writing tasks due to a lack of deep reflection and revision patterns. To address this, the authors introduce R2-Write, an automated framework that synthesizes high-quality thinking trajectories enriched with explicit reflection and revision patterns through iterative writer-judge interaction. A process reward mechanism is designed to improve reflection quality during reinforcement learning, enhancing both performance and token efficiency. Extensive experiments across multiple creative writing and deep-research benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements, validating the effectiveness of explicitly incorporating reflection and revision patterns for open-ended writing tasks.

**Title:** Prompt Compression in the Wild: Measuring Latency, Rate Adherence, and Quality for Faster LLM Inference

**Authors:** Cornelius Kummer, Lena Jurkschat, Michael Färber, Sahar Vahdati

**Abstract:** With the increasing use of language models in Information Retrieval (IR) and specifically RAG systems, the latency of large language models (LLMs) becomes a critical bottleneck due to long contexts leading to large prompts and increased computation. Prompt compression, which reduces prompt size while maintaining performance on downstream tasks, is a cost-effective method to accelerate inference in LLMs. The study evaluates the trade-off between preprocessing time and decoding speed across thousands of runs and 30,000 queries using several open-source LLMs and three GPU classes. LLMLingua achieves up to 18% end-to-end speed-ups when prompt length, compression ratio, and hardware capacity are well-matched, with no significant change in output quality. Outside this optimal range, the compression step outweighs the benefits. Effective compression can also reduce memory usage enough to move workloads from data center GPUs to commodity cards with minimal latency increase. The open-source profiler predicts the latency break-even point for each model-hardware setup, providing practical guidance on when prompt compression delivers real-world benefits.

**Subjects:** Information Retrieval (cs.IR); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Computation and Language (cs.CL)

Title: LogicPoison: Logical Attacks on Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Abstract: Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) enhances the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by grounding their responses in structured knowledge graphs. However, the security of GraphRAG systems relies on the topological integrity of the underlying graph, which can be compromised by corrupting logical connections without altering text semantics. The paper introduces **LogicPoison**, an attack framework that uses type-preserving entity swapping to perturb global logic hubs and query-specific reasoning bridges, disrupting graph connectivity and multi-hop inference paths. This approach maintains surface-level textual plausibility while degrading GraphRAG performance significantly, outperforming existing baselines in effectiveness and stealth.

Title: MSAO: Adaptive Modality Sparsity-Aware Offloading with Edge-Cloud Collaboration for Efficient Multimodal LLM Inference

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) enable powerful cross-modal reasoning capabilities but impose substantial computational and latency burdens, posing critical challenges for deployment on resource-constrained edge devices. This paper proposes MSAO, an adaptive modality sparsity-aware offloading framework with edge-cloud collaboration for efficient MLLM Inference. **Title:** Learning Task-Invariant Properties via Dreamer: Enabling Efficient Policy Transfer for Quadruped Robots

**Authors:** Junyang Liang, Yuxuan Liu, Yabin Chang, Junfan Lin, Junkai Ji, Hui Li, Changxin Huang, Jianqiang Li

**Abstract:** This paper introduces the DreamTIP framework to enhance sim-to-real transfer for quadruped robots. DreamTIP identifies and leverages Task-Invariant Properties, such as contact stability and terrain clearance, which are robust to dynamic variations and transferable across tasks. These properties are integrated into the world model as auxiliary prediction targets, enabling the policy to learn representations insensitive to underlying dynamic changes. Title: Analysis of Optimality of Large Language Models on Planning Problems

Abstract: Classic AI planning problems have been revisited in the Large Language Model (LLM) era, focusing on success rates rather than plan efficiency. The study examines whether frontier models reason optimally or rely on simple, inefficient strategies. The Blocksworld domain and the generalized Path-Star ($P^\*$) graph are used to isolate true topological reasoning from semantic priors. Reasoning-enhanced LLMs outperform traditional satisficing planners in complex, multi-goal configurations. LLMs track theoretical optimality limits with near-perfect precision, even without domain-specific semantic hints. The findings suggest two hypotheses: an active Algorithmic Simulation and a Geometric Memory that allows models to represent the $P^\*$ topology as a navigable global geometry, bypassing exponential combinatorial complexity.

Title: EvaNet: Towards More Efficient and Consistent Infrared and Visible Image Fusion Assessment

Abstract: Evaluation in image fusion research is crucial but existing metrics often lack adaptation and are computationally expensive. This framework decomposes the fusion result into infrared and visible components and measures information preservation. Experiments show EvaNet is up to 1,000 times faster and more consistent across benchmarks. Code will be available at this https URL.

Title: Progressive Video Condensation with MLLM Agent for Long-form Video Understanding

Abstract: Understanding long videos requires extracting query-relevant information from long sequences under tight compute budgets. Existing text-then-LLM pipelines lose fine-grained visual cues, while video-based multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can keep visual details but are too frame-hungry and computationally expensive. In this work, we propose ProVCA, a progressive video condensation agent that iteratively locates key video frames at multiple granularities. ProVCA achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot accuracies of 69.3% on EgoSchema, 80.5% on NExT-QA, and 77.7% on IntentQA, while using fewer frames than previous training-free methods.

Title: Token Warping Helps MLLMs Look from Nearby Viewpoints

Authors: Phillip Y. Lee, Chanho Park, Mingue Park, Seungwoo Yoo, Juil Koo, Minhyuk Sung

Abstract: The paper explores whether warping tokens rather than pixels can help multimodal large language models (MLLMs) understand scenes from nearby viewpoints. It finds that backward token warping, which defines a dense grid on the target view and retrieves corresponding source-view tokens, is more stable and preserves semantic coherence better than pixel-wise warping. Experiments on the proposed ViewBench benchmark show that token-level warping enables MLLMs to reason reliably from nearby viewpoints, outperforming all baselines.

**arXiv:2604.02852v1** (cs)

Title: Dependency-Guided Repository-Level C-to-Rust Translation with Reinforcement Alignment

Abstract: Automating C-to-Rust migration is critical for improving software security without sacrificing performance. Traditional rule-based methods struggle with diverse C idioms, often producing rigid and unidiomatic Rust code. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising alternative by leveraging cross-language generalization. However, challenges remain in handling cross-file dependencies, verifying syntactic correctness and functional equivalence, and the lack of large-scale C-Rust parallel data. 

We propose DepTrans, a framework that combines model capability enhancement with structured inference. DepTrans introduces Reinforcement-Aligned Syntax Training and Dependency-Guided Iterative Refinement to improve generation quality and capture fine-grained cross-file dependencies. Experiments show a 60.7% compilation success rate and 43.5% computational accuracy, outperforming the strongest baseline by 22.8 and 17.3 percentage points. It successfully builds 7 of 15 industrial C projects, demonstrating practical potential.

Subjects: Software Engineering (cs.SE)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.02852v1
**arXiv:2604.02816v1** (cs)

Title: QAPruner: Quantization-Aware Vision Token Pruning for Multimodal Large Language Models

Authors: Xinhao Wang, Zhonyu Xia, Zhiwei Lin, Zhe Li, Yongtao Wang

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) exhibit strong reasoning ability but face high computational and memory costs, hindering deployment in resource-constrained settings. Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and vision token pruning are standard compression techniques, but applying them independently can worsen quantization errors. The paper proposes a quantization-aware vision token pruning framework that introduces a hybrid sensitivity metric combining simulated group-wise quantization error with outlier intensity. This method retains tokens that are both semantically informative and robust to quantization. Experiments show improved accuracy over baseline methods, especially at aggressive pruning ratios.

Subjects: Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

Title: ChatSVA: Bridging SVA Generation for Hardware Verification via Task-Specific LLMs

Abstract: Functional verification of ICs consumes over 50% of the development lifecycle, where SystemVerilog Assertions (SVAs) are crucial. Manual SVA creation is labor-intensive and error-prone. ChatSVA, an end-to-end SVA generation system, addresses this by using a multi-agent framework to generate high-purity datasets. Evaluated on 24 RTL designs, ChatSVA achieves 98.66% syntax and 96.12% functional pass rates, generating 139.5 SVAs per design with 82.50% function coverage. This represents a 33.3 percentage point improvement in functional correctness and an over 11x enhancement in function coverage compared to the previous state-of-the-art. ChatSVA sets a new SOTA in automated SVA generation and is publicly available at the provided URL.

**Title:** Rubrics to Tokens: Bridging Response-level Rubrics and Token-level Rewards in Instruction Following Tasks

**Authors:** Tianze Xu, Yanzhao Zheng, Pengrui Lu, Lyumanshan Ye, Yong Wu, Zhentao Zhang, Yuanqiang Yu, Chao Ma, Jihuai Zhu, Pengfei Liu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang, Gang Yu

**Abstract:** Rubric-based Reinforcement Learning (RL) is proposed to align Large Language Models (LLMs) with complex, open-domain instruction following tasks. However, existing methods face reward sparsity and ambiguity issues due to reliance on response-level rewards. The paper introduces Rubrics to Tokens (RTT), a novel framework that bridges response-level scores and token-level credit assignment. RTT includes a Token-Level Relevance Discriminator and RTT-GRPO, which optimizes policy models by integrating response-level and token-level advantages. Additionally, a new normalization method, Intra-sample Token Group Normalization, is proposed to handle the transition to a three-dimensional reward space. Experiments show RTT outperforms baselines in instruction- and rubric-level accuracy across different models.

Title: Structure-Aware Commitment Reduction for Network-Constrained Unit Commitment with Solver-Preserving Guarantees

Abstract: The paper proposes a solver-compatible dimensionality reduction framework for network-constrained unit commitment (UC) that exploits structural regularities in commitment decisions. The framework identifies a sparse subset of structurally stable commitment binaries to fix prior to optimization, using an LLM to select these variables. This approach reduces the combinatorial burden without replacing the optimization process, achieving order-of-magnitude speedups on high-complexity instances while maintaining near-optimal objective values. Experiments on IEEE 57-bus, RTS 73-bus, IEEE 118-bus, and augmented large-scale cases demonstrate consistent reductions in branch-and-bound nodes and solution time.

Title: Improving Role Consistency in Multi-Agent Collaboration via Quantitative Role Clarity

Authors: Guoling Zhou, Wenpei Han, Fengqin Yang, Li Wang, Yingcong Zhou, Zhiguo Fu

Abstract: In large language model (LLM)-driven multi-agent systems, role specification failure is a major issue. The paper proposes a quantitative role clarity method to enhance role consistency. Title: Aligning Progress and Feasibility: A Neuro-Symbolic Dual Memory Framework for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential in long-horizon decision-making tasks but often struggle with endless trial-and-error loops or deviating from the main objective in complex environments. The authors attribute these issues to two fundamental errors: global Progress Drift and local Feasibility Violation. They propose a Neuro-Symbolic Dual Memory Framework that decouples semantic progress guidance from logical feasibility verification. This framework uses a neural-network-based Progress Memory and a symbolic-logic-based Feasibility Memory to guide task advancement and perform strict logical validation, respectively. Experiments show significant performance improvements on ALFWorld, WebShop, and TextCraft, reducing invalid action rates and average trajectory lengths.

* **Title:** Foundation Models Defining A New Era In Sensor-based Human Activity Recognition: A Survey And Outlook
* **Authors:** Sizhen Bian, Mengxi Liu, Siyu Yuan, Lala Shakti Swarup Ray, Bo Zhou, Bin Guo, Zhiwen Yu, Thomas Ploetz, Paul Lukowicz, Vitor Fortes Rey
* **Abstract:** Sensor-based Human Activity Recognition (HAR) is limited by scarce labels, sensor heterogeneity, and weak generalization. Foundation models, pretrained using self-supervised and multimodal learning, offer a unifying paradigm to address these challenges. This survey synthesizes emerging foundation models for sensor-based HAR, organizing existing work using a lifecycle-oriented taxonomy. It identifies three dominant development trajectories: (1) HAR-specific foundation models trained from scratch, (2) adaptation of general time-series or multimodal foundation models, and (3) integration of large language models. Open challenges include data curation, multimodal alignment, personalization, privacy, and responsible deployment.

**Title:** Finding Belief Geometries with Sparse Autoencoders

**Authors:** Matthew Levinson

**Abstract:** Understanding the geometric structure of internal representations is a central goal of mechanistic interpretability. This work explores whether large language models trained on naturalistic text develop geometric representations similar to those found in transformers trained on sequences generated by hidden Markov models. The authors introduce a pipeline combining sparse autoencoders (SAEs), $k$-subspace clustering, and simplex fitting to discover candidate simplex-structured subspaces in transformer representations. They validate the pipeline on a transformer trained on a multipartite hidden Markov model and apply it to Gemma-2-9B, identifying 13 priority clusters with candidate simplex geometry. Key challenges include distinguishing genuine belief-state encoding from tiling artifacts, which is addressed through barycentric prediction. Among the 13 clusters, 5 distinct real clusters pass at least one split, with one cluster achieving the highest causal steering score in the dataset.

Title: Steerable but Not Decodable: Function Vectors Operate Beyond the Logit Lens

Abstract: Function vectors (FVs) can steer large language model behavior when added to the residual stream. The study found that FV steering succeeds even when the logit lens cannot decode the correct answer. Key findings include:
- FV steering exceeds logit lens accuracy across all tasks and models.
- FVs encode computational instructions rather than answer directions.
- Mistral FVs rewrite intermediate representations, while Llama/Gemma FVs produce near-zero changes despite successful steering.

Title: Do Audio-Visual Large Language Models Really See and Hear?

Authors: Ramaneswaran Selvakumar, Kaousheik Jayakumar, S Sakshi, Sreyan Ghosh, Ruohan Gao, Dinesh Manocha

Abstract: Audio-Visual Large Language Models (AVLLMs) are emerging as unified interfaces for multimodal perception. This study is the first mechanistic interpretability analysis of AVLLMs, examining how audio and visual features evolve and fuse through different layers to produce final text outputs. The study finds that while AVLLMs encode rich audio semantics at intermediate layers, these capabilities fail to manifest in the final text generation when audio conflicts with vision. Probing analyses reveal that useful latent audio information is present but deeper fusion layers disproportionately favor visual representations, suppressing audio cues. The imbalance is traced to training, where the AVLLM's audio behavior closely matches its vision-language base model, indicating limited additional alignment to audio supervision. The findings highlight a fundamental modality bias in AVLLMs and provide new insights into how multimodal LLMs integrate audio and vision.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Sound (cs.SD)

Cite as: arXiv:2604.02605 [cs.AI]

**Title:** An Empirical Study of Many-Shot In-Context Learning for Machine Translation of Low-Resource Languages

**Authors:** Yinhan Lu, Gaganpreet Jhajj, Chen Zhang, Anietie Andy, David Ifeoluwa Adelani

**Abstract:** In-context learning (ICL) enables large language models (LLMs) to adapt to new tasks with a few examples, which is promising for underrepresented languages. This study explores many-shot ICL for machine translation from English to ten low-resource languages. Key findings include:
- Many-shot ICL becomes more effective with an increase in examples.
- BM25-based retrieval significantly improves data efficiency: 50 retrieved examples are comparable to 250 many-shot examples, and 250 retrieved examples perform similarly to 1,000 many-shot examples.

**Title:** AI Fact-Checking in the Wild: A Field Evaluation of LLM-Written Community Notes on X

**Authors:** Haiwen Li, Michiel A. Bakker

**Abstract:**
Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate promising capabilities for contextual fact-checking on social media. They can verify contested claims through deep research, synthesize evidence from multiple sources, and draft explanations at scale. The LLM writer, a multi-step pipeline handling multimodal content and conducting web and platform-native search, was deployed to write 1,614 notes on 1,597 tweets and compared against 1,332 human-written notes using 108,169 ratings from 42,521 raters. Title: High Volatility and Action Bias Distinguish LLMs from Humans in Group Coordination

**arXiv:2604.02556v1** (cs)

Title: Fast NF4 Dequantization Kernels for Large Language Model Inference

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) require quantization techniques for practical deployment due to memory constraints. NF4 (4-bit NormalFloat) quantization reduces memory usage by 4 times but requires expensive dequantization to FP16 format for inference on NVIDIA GPUs, creating a performance bottleneck. This paper introduces a lightweight shared memory optimization that achieves 2.0--2.2 times kernel speedup across three models (Gemma 27B, Qwen3 32B, and Llama3.3 70B) and up to 1.54 times end-to-end improvement by leveraging shared memory's latency advantage over global memory. The optimization uses only 64 bytes of shared memory per thread block and simplifies indexing logic, demonstrating substantial performance gains with minimal engineering effort. This work provides a plug-and-play solution for the HuggingFace ecosystem.

Title: Beyond Resolution Rates: Behavioral Drivers of Coding Agent Success and Failure

Authors: Tural Mehtiyev, Wesley Assunção

Abstract: Coding agents, combining Large Language Models (LLMs) with tool-augmented interaction loops, face severe limitations despite their reasoning capabilities. Top-ranked LLM-based coding agents fail on over 20% of benchmarked problems. The study analyzes 9,374 trajectories from 19 agents on 500 tasks, identifying that patch complexity alone does not explain difficulty. Agents fail due to gaps in architectural reasoning and domain knowledge. Behavioral patterns differentiate success from failure: agents that gather context before editing and invest in validation succeed more often. The LLM is the primary driver of both outcome and behavior, with agents sharing the same LLM agreeing on more tasks than those sharing the same framework. Framework prompts influence agent tactics, but this influence diminishes with stronger LLMs.

Title: Synapse: Evolving Job-Person Fit with Explainable Two-phase Retrieval and LLM-guided Genetic Resume Optimization

Abstract: Modern recruitment platforms face severe information imbalance, with job seekers searching through vast, rapidly changing job postings and employers dealing with high-volume, low-relevance applicant pools. Title: PolyJarvis: LLM Agent for Autonomous Polymer MD Simulations

Abstract: PolyJarvis is an agent that uses a large language model (LLM) and the RadonPy simulation platform to predict polymer properties from natural language input. Title: AdaHOP: Fast and Accurate Low-Precision Training via Outlier-Pattern-Aware Rotation

Authors: Seonggon Kim, Alireza Khodamoradi, Kristof Denolf, Eunhyeok Park

Abstract: Low-precision training (LPT) often uses Hadamard transforms to reduce outliers and quantization error in large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods apply a fixed transform uniformly, which is ineffective due to varying outlier structures across tensors. The paper introduces AdaHOP, which identifies three types of outlier patterns (Row-wise, Column-wise, and None) and applies tailored strategies to minimize quantization error. AdaHOP achieves BF16 training quality at MXFP4 precision, offering up to 3.6X memory compression and 1.8X kernel acceleration compared to BF16 full-precision training.

**Title:** Social Meaning in Large Language Models: Structure, Magnitude, and Pragmatic Prompting

**Authors:** Roland Mühlenbernd

**Abstract:**
Large language models (LLMs) increasingly exhibit human-like patterns of pragmatic and social reasoning. This paper introduces two calibration-focused metrics: the Effect Size Ratio (ESR) and the Calibration Deviation Score (CDS) to assess whether LLMs approximate human social meaning both qualitatively and quantitatively. The study also explores prompting strategies based on pragmatic theory to improve this approximation. The findings indicate that while all models reliably reproduce the qualitative structure of human social inferences, they differ in magnitude calibration. Prompting models to reason about speaker knowledge and motives most consistently reduces magnitude deviation, while prompting for alternative-awareness tends to amplify exaggeration. Combining both components improves calibration-sensitive metrics across all models, though fine-grained magnitude calibration remains unresolved.

Title: Batched Contextual Reinforcement: A Task-Scaling Law for Efficient Reasoning

Abstract: Large Language Models using Chain-of-Thought reasoning face high token consumption costs. The paper introduces Batched Contextual Reinforcement (BCR), a training method that allows models to solve multiple problems simultaneously within a shared context window, reducing token usage while maintaining or improving accuracy. Key findings include:
1. A task-scaling law where increasing the number of concurrent problems (N) decreases per-problem token usage.
2. BCR reduces token usage by 15.8% to 62.6% across model families while maintaining or improving accuracy.
3. Models exhibit self-regulated efficiency, eliminating redundant loops without explicit supervision.
4. Implicit budget constraints prevent adversarial gradients and optimization collapse, offering a stable alternative to explicit length penalties.

**Title:** Unifying Group-Relative and Self-Distillation Policy Optimization via Sample Routing

Title: Answering the Wrong Question: Reasoning Trace Inversion for Abstention in LLMs

Abstract: For Large Language Models (LLMs) to be reliably deployed, models must effectively know when not to answer: abstain. The authors propose a Query Misalignment Framework to address the issue of poor abstention abilities in reasoning models. They develop a new class of state-of-the-art abstention methods called Trace Inversion, which involves generating the reasoning trace of a model, reconstructing the most likely query, and comparing it with the initial query. **Title:** Measuring What Cannot Be Surveyed: LLMs as Instruments for Latent Cognitive Variables in Labor Economics

**Authors:** Cristian Espinal Maya

**Abstract:**
This paper establishes theoretical and practical foundations for using Large Language Models (LLMs) as measurement instruments for latent economic variables, particularly those describing the cognitive content of occupational tasks at a level of granularity not achievable with existing survey instruments. The author formalizes four conditions for valid instruments: semantic exogeneity, construct relevance, monotonicity, and model invariance. The Augmented Human Capital Index (AHC\_o) is constructed from 18,796 O\*NET task statements scored by Claude Haiku 4.5 and validated against six existing AI exposure indices. The index shows strong convergent validity (r = 0.85 with Eloundou GPT-gamma, r = 0.79 with Felten AIOE) and discriminant validity. Principal component analysis confirms that AI-related occupational measures span two distinct dimensions: augmentation and substitution. Inter-rater reliability across two LLM models yields Pearson r = 0.76 and Krippendorff's alpha = 0.71. Prompt sensitivity analysis shows robust task-level rankings. Obviously Related Instrumental Variables (ORIV) estimation recovers coefficients 25% larger than OLS, consistent with classical measurement error attenuation. The methodology generalizes beyond labor economics to any domain requiring semantic content quantification at scale.

**Title:** Impact of Multimodal and Conversational AI on Learning Outcomes and Experience

**Authors:** Karan Taneja, Anjali Singh, Ashok K. Goel

**Abstract:**
Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) support multimedia learning through conversational systems grounded in educational content. The study compares three approaches to learning biology from textbook content: (1) MuDoC (document-grounded conversational AI with interleaved text-and-image responses), (2) TexDoC (document-grounded conversational AI with text-only responses), and (3) DocSearch (textbook interface with semantic search and highlighting). **Title:** Towards Position-Robust Talent Recommendation via Large Language Models

**Abstract:** Talent recruitment is costly and time-consuming. Current talent recommendation systems use large language models (LLMs) due to their language understanding capabilities. However, existing approaches have limitations such as high token consumption and position bias. To address these, the paper introduces L3TR, a novel framework that uses a block attention mechanism and local positional encoding to enhance inter-document processing and mitigate position bias. It also includes an ID sampling method for consistent candidate set sizes and debiasing methods. Experiments on real-world datasets show consistent improvements over existing methods.

Title: Neuro-RIT: Neuron-Guided Instruction Tuning for Robust Retrieval-Augmented Language Model

Authors: Jaemin Kim, Jae O Lee, Sumyeong Ahn, Seo Yeon Park

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Language Models (RALMs) are shown to be effective in knowledge-intensive tasks but are vulnerable to performance degradation with irrelevant or noisy contexts. Neuro-RIT (Neuron-guided Robust Instruction Tuning) is proposed to enhance robustness by disentangling neurons responsible for relevant versus irrelevant contexts and introducing a two-stage instruction tuning strategy for noise robustness and evidence distillation. Experiments show consistent outperformance over strong baselines and robustness methods.

Title: The Expert Strikes Back: Interpreting Mixture-of-Experts Language Models at Expert Level

Authors: Jeremy Herbst, Jae Hee Lee, Stefan Wermter

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures are used for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) by activating only a subset of parameters per token. The study compares MoE experts and dense feed-forward networks (FFNs) using $k$-sparse probing, finding that MoE experts are less polysemantic and more monosemantic as routing becomes sparser. This suggests that MoE experts specialize in fine-grained tasks such as linguistic operations or semantic tasks. The findings indicate that MoE models are inherently interpretable at the expert level, providing a clearer path toward large-scale model interpretability. Code is available at: this https URL

**Title:** TRACE-Bot: Detecting Emerging LLM-Driven Social Bots via Implicit Semantic Representations and AIGC-Enhanced Behavioral Patterns

**Authors:** Zhongbo Wang, Zhiyu Lin, Zhu Wang, Haizhou Wang

**Abstract:**
Large Language Model (LLM)-driven social bots pose a growing threat by generating human-like content that evades conventional detection. Existing methods struggle with limited detection accuracy due to overreliance on single-modality signals, insufficient sensitivity to AIGC generative patterns, and inadequate modeling of the interplay between linguistic patterns and behavioral dynamics. To address these issues, the authors propose TRACE-Bot, a unified dual-channel framework that jointly models implicit semantic representations and AIGC-enhanced behavioral patterns. TRACE-Bot constructs fine-grained representations from personal information, interaction behavior, and tweet data. It uses a dual-channel architecture to capture linguistic representations via a pretrained language model and behavioral irregularities via multidimensional activity features augmented with signals from state-of-the-art AIGC detectors. The framework achieves SOTA performance with accuracies of 98.46% and 97.50% on two public datasets, demonstrating robustness against advanced bot strategies.

**Title:** GaelEval: Benchmarking LLM Performance for Scottish Gaelic

**Authors:** Peter Devine, William Lamb, Beatrice Alex, Ignatius Ezeani, Dawn Knight, Mícheál J. Ó Meachair, Paul Rayson, Martin Wynne

**Abstract:** Multilingual large language models (LLMs) often exhibit emergent 'shadow' capabilities in languages without official support, yet their performance on these languages remains uneven and under-measured. GaelEval introduces a multi-dimensional benchmark for Scottish Gaelic, including: (i) an expert-authored morphosyntactic MCQA task; (ii) a culturally grounded translation benchmark; and (iii) a large-scale cultural knowledge Q&A task. Gemini 3 Pro Preview achieves 83.3% accuracy on the linguistic task, surpassing the human baseline (78.1%). Proprietary models outperform open-weight systems, and in-language prompting yields a small but stable advantage (+2.4%). Leading models exceed 90% accuracy on cultural tasks, though most systems perform worse under Gaelic prompting.

**arXiv:2604.06170v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: The paper introduces Paper Circle, an open-source multi-agent research discovery and analysis framework aimed at reducing the effort required to find, assess, organize, and understand academic literature. It consists of two pipelines:
1. Discovery Pipeline: Integrates offline and online retrieval from multiple sources, uses multi-criteria scoring, diversity-aware ranking, and provides structured outputs.
2. Analysis Pipeline: Transforms individual papers into structured knowledge graphs with typed nodes like concepts, methods, experiments, and figures, enabling graph-aware question answering and coverage verification.

The system is implemented using a coder LLM-based multi-agent orchestration framework, producing fully reproducible, synchronized outputs in formats such as JSON, CSV, BibTeX, Markdown, and HTML. Evaluation benchmarks include paper retrieval and paper review generation, measuring hit rate, MRR, and Recall at K. Results indicate consistent improvements with stronger agent models.

**arXiv:2604.06169v1**

Abstract: The traditional "train then deploy" approach restricts Large Language Models (LLMs) from dynamically adjusting their weights based on continuous real-world data. This paper introduces In-Place Test-Time Training (In-Place TTT), which allows LLMs to update certain parameters during inference. It specifically targets the final projection matrix of MLP blocks as adaptable fast weights, enhancing models without full retraining. The method also refines the update objective to better align with language modeling tasks, leading to improved performance on large-context tasks. Experiments show that a 4B-parameter model using In-Place TTT performs well on tasks involving up to 128k context length.

**arXiv:2604.06159v1** (cs)

Abstract: In Reinforcement Learning (RL), given a prompt, the model samples a set of completions and scores them. The paper introduces *Target Policy Optimization* (TPO), which separates the process into two parts: constructing a target distribution based on the scored completions and fitting the policy to this target using cross-entropy. TPO performs well on tabular bandits, transformer sequence tasks, and large language models under sparse rewards, matching or outperforming standard methods like Policy Gradient (PG), Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Generalized Reward-Prediction Optimizer (GRPO), and Distributional Gradient (DG).

Title: MMEmb-R1: Reasoning-Enhanced Multimodal Embedding with Pair-Aware Selection and Adaptive Control

**Title:** Toward Consistent World Models with Multi-Token Prediction and Latent Semantic Enhancement

**Abstract:** The paper discusses the development of coherent internal world models in Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) as a promising approach for learning structured representations compared to traditional Next-Token Prediction (NTP). The authors analyze the gradient inductive bias of MTP, providing empirical evidence that supports its effectiveness in promoting convergence towards internal belief states through gradient coupling. However, they highlight that standard MTP can suffer from structural hallucinations due to illegal shortcuts in latent space. To address this issue, the paper proposes Latent Semantic Enhancement MTP (LSE-MTP), which aligns predictions with ground-truth hidden state trajectories. Experimental results on synthetic graphs and real-world Manhattan Taxi Ride data demonstrate that LSE-MTP improves representation alignment, reduces structural hallucinations, and enhances robustness to perturbations.

Claw-Eval: Toward Trustworthy Evaluation of Autonomous Agents

This paper introduces Claw-Eval, an evaluation suite designed to assess autonomous agents in real-world software environments. It addresses three main limitations of current benchmarks: opaque trajectory grading, underspecified safety and robustness evaluations, and limited modality coverage. The suite includes 300 human-verified tasks across nine categories, recording every agent action through three evidence channels. Scoring focuses on Completion, Safety, and Robustness, providing metrics like Average Score, Pass@k, and Pass^k to distinguish genuine capabilities from luck. Experiments show that trajectory-opaque evaluations miss significant safety and robustness issues, and multimodal performance varies widely among models.

Title:PoM: A Linear-Time Replacement for Attention with the Polynomial Mixer

Abstract: This paper introduces the Polynomial Mixer (PoM), a novel token mixing mechanism with linear complexity that acts as a drop-in replacement for self-attention. PoM uses a learned polynomial function to aggregate input tokens into a compact representation from which each token retrieves contextual information. It ensures that transformers with PoM remain universal sequence-to-sequence approximators. The method was tested in five diverse domains: text generation, handwritten text recognition, image generation, 3D modeling, and Earth observation. PoM matches the performance of attention-based models while significantly reducing computational costs for long sequences.

**arXiv:2604.06126v1** introduces *Gym-Anything*, a framework designed to convert any software into an interactive environment for computer-use agents. This framework addresses the challenge of creating environments for complex software, which traditionally requires significant time and human effort. Key aspects include:

- **Multi-Agent Task Framework**: Involves a coding agent responsible for writing setup scripts, downloading real-world data, configuring software, and providing evidence of correct setup. An audit agent independently verifies the setup against a quality checklist.
  
- **Economic Value Occupation Taxonomy**: Utilizes a taxonomy based on U.S. GDP data to apply the framework to 200 software applications across various occupations. The result is *CUA-World*, comprising over 10,000 long-horizon tasks across diverse fields like medical science, astronomy, engineering, and enterprise systems.

- **Benchmarking**: Includes *CUA-World-Long*, a challenging benchmark with tasks requiring over 500 steps. Training data distilled into a 2 billion parameter vision-language model outperforms larger models. At test time, a separate vision-language model improves performance on *CUA-World-Long*.

This research aims to advance the development of realistic computer-use agents through scalable and efficient environment creation methods.

Title: ACE-Bench: Agent Configurable Evaluation with Scalable Horizons and Controllable Difficulty under Lightweight Environments

Abstract: Existing agent benchmarks suffer from high environment interaction overhead and imbalanced task horizon and difficulty distributions, making aggregate scores unreliable. The proposed ACE-Bench addresses these issues by offering a unified grid-based planning task where agents must fill hidden slots in a partially completed schedule under local and global constraints. It provides fine-grained control through two orthogonal axes: Scalable Horizons (controlled by the number of hidden slots $H$) and Controllable Difficulty (governed by a decoy budget $B$). All tool calls are resolved via static JSON files in a Lightweight Environment, enabling fast and reproducible evaluations suitable for training-time validation.

The study validates the reliability of control over task horizon and difficulty using $H$ and $B$. Comprehensive experiments across 13 diverse models reveal significant performance variations and confirm that ACE-Bench provides interpretable and controllable evaluation of agent reasoning.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.06111v1

\[Submitted on 7 Apr 2026\]

Title: Pixel-Translation-Equivariant Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks via Fourier Multiplexers

Authors: Dmitry Chirkov, Igor Lobanov

Abstract: Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) benefit greatly from hard-coded translation equivariance. This paper proposes Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks (QCNNs) as near-term quantum analogues. The key challenge addressed is the mismatch between different data encodings and the required translation equivariance. Specifically, for address/amplitude encodings like FRQI, a pixel shift corresponds to modular addition on an index register. In contrast, many Multi-scale Entanglement Renormalization Ansatz (MERA)-inspired QCNNs are only equivariant under cyclic permutations of physical qubits. The authors formalize this issue and construct QCNN layers that exactly commute with the pixel cyclic shift (PCS) symmetry induced by the encoding. They demonstrate that any PCS-equivariant layer can be characterized as a Fourier-mode multiplexer followed by an inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (IQFT).

The paper introduces a deep PCS-QCNN architecture with measurement-induced pooling, deferred conditioning, and inter-layer QFT cancellation. Additionally, it analyzes the trainability of the model at random initialization and proves a lower bound on the expected squared gradient norm that remains constant in a depth-scaling regime, thereby avoiding a depth-induced barren plateau.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.06094v1

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents act as human delegates in multi-agent environments, where a representative agent integrates diverse peer perspectives to make decisions. Experiments show that increased social pressure, such as larger adversarial groups, more capable peers, and longer arguments, leads to significant performance degradation. Rhetorical strategies emphasizing credibility or logic further influence the agent’s judgment based on context.

**arXiv:2604.06086v1**

Abstract: This paper introduces LAG-XAI (Lie Affine Geometry for Explainable AI), a novel geometric framework that models paraphrasing as a structured affine transformation within the embedding space rather than discrete word substitutions. It proposes a computationally efficient mean-field approximation inspired by local Lie group actions, which decomposes paraphrase transitions into geometrically interpretable components: rotation, deformation, and translation. Experiments show an AUC of 0.7713 on the PIT-2015 Twitter corpus. The model captures approximately 80% of the non-linear baseline's effective classification capacity while offering explicit parametric interpretability. It identifies fundamental geometric invariants such as a stable matrix reconfiguration angle (~27.84°) and near-zero deformation, indicating local isometry. The practical utility of LAG-XAI is demonstrated in detecting LLM hallucinations, achieving 95.3% detection accuracy on the HaluEval dataset.

**arXiv:2604.06074v1**

Title: Graph-PiT: Enhancing Structural Coherence in Part-Based Image Synthesis via Graph Priors

Abstract: Achieving fine-grained and structurally sound controllability is essential for advanced visual generation. Current part-based frameworks treat user-provided parts as an unordered set, ignoring their inherent spatial and semantic relationships, leading to compositions lacking structural integrity. To address this issue, the authors propose Graph-PiT, a framework that models the structural dependencies of visual components using a graph prior. This involves representing visual parts as nodes and their spatial-semantic relationships as edges. The core of the method is a Hierarchical Graph Neural Network (HGNN) module performing bidirectional message passing between coarse-grained part-level super-nodes and fine-grained IP+ token sub-nodes, refining part embeddings before they enter the generative pipeline. Additionally, the authors introduce a graph Laplacian smoothness loss and an edge-reconstruction loss to ensure adjacent parts have compatible, relationship-aware embeddings. Experiments conducted on controlled synthetic domains (characters, products, indoor layouts, and jigsaw puzzles) and transferred to real web images demonstrate that Graph-PiT improves structural coherence compared to vanilla PiT while maintaining compatibility with the original IP-Prior pipeline. Ablation studies confirm that explicit relational reasoning is crucial for enforcing user-specified adjacency constraints. The approach enhances the plausibility of generated concepts and provides a scalable and interpretable mechanism for complex, multi-part image synthesis. The research was accepted by ICME 2026 and the code is available at the provided link.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.06074v1

Title: Short Data, Long Context: Distilling Positional Knowledge in Transformers

Abstract: Extending the context window of language models typically requires expensive long-context pre-training, posing significant challenges for both training efficiency and data collection. This paper demonstrates that long-context retrieval capabilities can be transferred to student models through logit-based knowledge distillation, even when training exclusively on packed short-context samples within a long-context window. Key findings include:
1. Phase-wise RoPE scaling maximizes rotational spectrum utilization at each training stage, achieving the best long-context performance.
2. Logit-based knowledge distillation enables direct positional information transfer.
3. Structured update patterns in the query state during long-context extension reveal sensitivity to long-context training.

Subjects: Computation and Language (cs.CL), Machine Learning (cs.LG)

**arXiv:2604.06066v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: Intrinsic self-correction in Large Language Models (LLMs) often fails in open-ended reasoning tasks due to "hallucination snowballing," where models recursively justify early errors. Structured feedback can help, but current methods rely on external tools, reducing autonomy. This study examines if structured reflection through outline-based constrained decoding can reduce error propagation without extra training. Using an 8-billion-parameter model (Qwen3-8B), researchers found that imposing structural constraints did not improve self-correction but triggered "structure snowballing." This occurs because strict formatting demands increase cognitive load, leading to formatting issues. The study reveals an "alignment tax" in constrained decoding, indicating a conflict between structural detail and model capacity in autonomous processes. Code and logs are available at [GitHub repository](this https URL).
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.06066v1
**arXiv:2604.06036v1**

Title: CoStream: Codec-Guided Resource-Efficient System for Video Streaming Analytics

Abstract: Video streaming analytics is critical for vision-language model serving but faces high costs due to multimodal inference. Previous systems reduce costs by leveraging temporal and spatial redundancies in videos but focus narrowly on either vision transformers (ViTs) or large language models (LLMs). These systems also suffer from significant overheads in identifying redundancies, making them unsuitable for dynamic real-time streams. 

CoStream addresses these issues by utilizing metadata from video codecs, which inherently capture temporal and spatial structures during compression. It uses this metadata to optimize video decoding, visual processing, and LLM prefilling without requiring offline training. Key optimizations include codec-guided patch pruning before ViT encoding and selective key-value cache refreshing during LLM prefilling. 

Experiments demonstrate that CoStream improves throughput by up to 3x and reduces GPU compute by up to 87%, while maintaining competitive accuracy with only a 0-8% F1 score drop compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

Subjects: Distributed, Parallel, and Cluster Computing (cs.DC), Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV), Machine Learning (cs.LG)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.06036v1

Title: Arch: An AI-Native Hardware Description Language for Register-Transfer Clocked Hardware Design

Abstract: We introduce Arch, a hardware description language (HDL) specifically designed for AI-assisted code generation and micro-architecture specification. Arch includes built-in language constructs for various hardware components such as pipelines, finite state machines (FSMs), FIFOs, arbiters, register files, buses, and clock-domain crossings. These constructs are designed to reduce errors commonly found in traditional HDLs.

Key Features:
- Clocks and resets are treated as parameterized types, enabling compile-time checks for clock-domain crossing (CDC) and reset-domain crossing (RDC).
- The type system ensures correctness by checking for issues like multiple drivers, undriven ports, width mismatches, and unsynchronized domain crossings.
- The language is structured to be easily generated by large language models (LLMs) from natural language descriptions.
- The Arch compiler outputs clean SystemVerilog code compliant with IEEE 1800-2017 standards and integrates a simulation toolchain for accurate simulation.

Case Studies:
- An 8-way set-associative L1 data cache.
- A synthesizable PG021-compatible AXI DMA controller.

Comparison:
- Arch is compared to other modern HDLs including SystemVerilog, VHDL, Chisel, and Bluespec in terms of expressiveness, safety, and AI suitability.

Subjects: Programming Languages (cs.PL); Computation and Language (cs.CL)

ACM classes: D.3; B.5

Title:QiMeng-PRepair: Precise Code Repair via Edit-Aware Reward Optimization

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often suffer from over-editing in program repair tasks. This paper introduces PRepair, a framework designed to mitigate over-editing and improve repair accuracy. It consists of two components: Self-Breaking, which generates buggy programs through controlled bug injection; and Self-Repairing, which trains models using Edit-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (EA-GRPO) with an edit-aware reward to encourage minimal yet correct edits. Experiments show that PRepair improves repair precision by up to 31.4% under the $\\mathrm{fix}\_1@1$ metric, which evaluates both repair correctness and extent. Additionally, it significantly increases decoding throughput when combined with speculative editing.

**arXiv:2604.05942v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: Post-training hybridization of large language models (LLMs) often involves replacing quadratic self-attention with sliding-window attention (SWA) to reduce KV cache usage and improve latency. The proposed method, BOSCH (Black-box Binary Optimization for Short-context Attention-Head Selection), is a training-free approach that optimizes short-context attention-head selection using Large Neighborhood Search. Title: Context-Value-Action Architecture for Value-Driven Large Language Model Agents

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) show promise in simulating human behavior but often exhibit behavioral rigidity. Evaluating LLMs against empirical ground truth reveals that increasing prompt-driven reasoning does not improve fidelity but instead exacerbates value polarization and reduces population diversity. The authors propose the Context-Value-Action (CVA) architecture, based on the Stimulus-Organism-Response (S-O-R) model and Schwartz's Theory of Basic Human Values. Unlike self-verifying methods, CVA uses a Value Verifier trained on authentic human data to model dynamic value activation. **arXiv:2604.05931v1**

Abstract: Zero-shot unsupervised reinforcement learning (URL) is explored for creating generalist agents that can generalize to unseen tasks without additional supervision. Successor representations (SR) are effective in structured, low-dimensional settings but struggle in high-dimensional visual environments. The study identifies two key limitations of SR in visual URL: (1) SR objectives often result in suboptimal representations that focus on irrelevant dynamics, leading to inaccurate successor measures and poor task generalization; (2) flawed representations hinder SR policies from accurately modeling multi-modal skill-conditioned action distributions and ensuring skill controllability.

To address these issues, the authors propose Saliency-Guided Representation with Consistency Policy Learning (SRCP). This framework separates representation learning from successor training by introducing a saliency-guided dynamics task to capture relevant dynamics, thus enhancing successor measures and task generalization. Additionally, it incorporates a fast-sampling consistency policy with URL-specific classifier-free guidance and tailored training objectives to improve policy modeling and controllability.

Experiments conducted on 16 tasks across 4 datasets from the ExORL benchmark show that SRCP achieves state-of-the-art performance in zero-shot generalization for visual URL and is adaptable to various SR methods.

Title:Swiss-Bench 003: Evaluating LLM Reliability and Adversarial Security for Swiss Regulatory Contexts

Abstract:The deployment of large language models (LLMs) in Swiss financial and regulatory contexts requires empirical evidence of production reliability and adversarial security. The paper introduces Swiss-Bench 003 (SBP-003), which extends the HAAS (Helvetic AI Assessment Score) from six to eight dimensions by adding D7 (Self-Graded Reliability Proxy) and D8 (Adversarial Security). **arXiv:2604.05868v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) exhibit strong performance on challenging tasks like math and coding. This paper compares parallel and sequential sampling methods used in LRMs. It finds that parallel sampling generally outperforms sequential sampling despite having less representational capacity. Three hypotheses are proposed to explain this discrepancy: (i) the effectiveness of the aggregator operator in parallel sampling, (ii) the disadvantage of sequential sampling due to longer context requirements, and (iii) reduced exploration in sequential sampling because of conditioning on past answers. Empirical evidence from various model families and sizes (Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1 distilled models, Gemini 2.5) and different question domains (math and coding) indicates that insufficient exploration is a significant factor contributing to the performance gap between the two sampling methods.

Title: Beyond Paper-to-Paper: Structured Profiling and Rubric Scoring for Paper-Reviewer Matching

Abstract: As conference submission volumes increase, accurately recommending suitable reviewers has become challenging. Current methods rely on "Paper-to-Paper" matching, which inadequately captures multi-dimensional expertise. The proposed framework, P2R, shifts to explicit profile-based matching using general-purpose LLMs to create structured profiles for submissions and reviewers. These profiles are broken down into Topics, Methodologies, and Applications. P2R employs a coarse-to-fine pipeline combining semantic and aspect-level signals for efficient and thorough candidate evaluation. Experiments on NeurIPS, SIGIR, and SciRepEval demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods.

**arXiv:2604.05865v1** (cs)

Abstract: This paper introduces JTON (JSON Tabular Object Notion), a JSON superset designed to reduce token redundancy when large language models (LLMs) process structured data. The core concept, "Zen Grid," minimizes repeated key names by factoring column headers into a single row and encoding values using semicolons. This approach maintains JSON's type system while significantly reducing token usage. In various real-world applications, Zen Grid reduced token counts by 15-60% compared to standard JSON compact formats. Tests across ten LLMs showed a slight improvement in accuracy (+0.3 pp) and full syntactic validity in generation tasks. Additionally, a Rust/PyO3 implementation offers faster parsing speeds (1.4x) than Python's json module.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Programming Languages (cs.PL)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05865v1
**arXiv:2604.05863v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: We introduce LoRM (Language of Rotating Machinery), a self-supervised framework designed for understanding multi-modal rotating machinery signals and performing real-time condition monitoring. This framework treats rotating machinery signals as a machine language, converting local signals into discrete symbolic units and predicting their future evolution based on observed multi-sensor context. Unlike traditional signal processing techniques, LoRM reformulates sensor data into a token-based sequence prediction problem. It retains observed context segments in continuous form while quantizing future target segments into discrete tokens. Efficient knowledge transfer is achieved through partial fine-tuning of a pre-trained language model on industrial signals, eliminating the need for training a large model from scratch. Condition monitoring is conducted by tracking token-prediction errors, which serve as indicators of the machinery's health status; increasing errors suggest degradation. Experiments show stable real-time tracking and strong cross-tool generalization, indicating that LoRM bridges language modeling and industrial signal analysis effectively. The source code is publicly accessible.

Title: When Do We Need LLMs? A Diagnostic for Language-Driven Bandits

Title: Deep Researcher Agent: An Autonomous Framework for 24/7 Deep Learning Experimentation with Zero-Cost Monitoring

Abstract: We introduce **Deep Researcher Agent**, an open-source framework designed for autonomous deep learning experimentation. This framework includes:

Title: Evaluating Learner Representations for Differentiation Prior to Instructional Outcomes

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly relying on agentic capabilities such as iterative retrieval, tool use, and decision-making to overcome the limitations of static, parametric knowledge. Title: BodhiPromptShield: Pre-Inference Prompt Mediation for Suppressing Privacy Propagation in LLM/VLM Agents

Abstract: In large language models (LLM) and vision-language models (VLM), prompt privacy risks propagate beyond a single model call due to raw user content flowing into retrieval queries, memory writes, tool calls, and logs. Current de-identification pipelines handle document boundaries but do not address cross-stage propagation. The authors propose BodhiPromptShield, a policy-aware framework that detects sensitive content, routes it through typed placeholders, semantic abstraction, or secure symbolic mapping, and delays restoration until authorized boundaries. This method enhances enterprise redaction by adding propagation-aware mediation and restoration timing as a security variable. Evaluation on the Controlled Prompt-Privacy Benchmark (CPPB) shows suppression rates ranging from 10.7% to 7.1% across retrieval, memory, and tool stages, with a PER score of 9.3%, AC of 0.94, and TSR of 0.92, outperforming generic de-identification methods.

Title: RHVI-FDD: A Hierarchical Decoupling Framework for Low-Light Image Enhancement

Abstract: This paper introduces a novel hierarchical decoupling framework (RHVI-FDD) designed to enhance low-light images. The framework addresses issues such as noise, detail loss, and color distortion in low-light conditions. It employs a RHVI transform at the macro level to decouple luminance and chrominance robustly. At the micro level, it uses a Frequency-Domain Decoupling (FDD) module to separate features into low, mid, and high-frequency bands, which are processed separately and then recombined using an adaptive gating module. Experiments show superior performance compared to existing methods in both objective metrics and visual quality.

**arXiv:2604.05773v1**

Abstract: Multimodal learning has gained significant attention due to its practical applications but often faces issues with insufficient optimization, leading to underperformance compared to unimodal models. The authors argue that balanced learning is not always optimal for multimodal learning; instead, prioritizing the performance-dominant modality (the one showing superior unimodal performance) can enhance overall multimodal performance. They introduce the Performance-Dominant Modality Prioritization (PDMP) strategy which identifies the most effective modality through performance rankings of independently trained unimodal models. PDMP then adjusts gradients using asymmetric coefficients to emphasize the performance-dominant modality during optimization. This approach is independent of multimodal model structures and fusion methods, making it versatile for various practical scenarios. Extensive experiments across different datasets confirm the effectiveness of PDMP in improving multimodal learning outcomes.

Title: CAKE: Cloud Architecture Knowledge Evaluation of Large Language Models

Title:Hackers or Hallucinators? A Comprehensive Analysis of LLM-Based Automated Penetration Testing

Abstract:The paper discusses the advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and their application in Automated Penetration Testing (AutoPT). It highlights the lack of systematic architectural analysis and large-scale empirical comparisons among existing studies. **arXiv:2604.05718v1** (cs)

Abstract: Decreasing sequence length is a common method to accelerate transformers, but previous token reduction techniques often focus on classification tasks and report proxy metrics instead of end-to-end latency. For semantic segmentation, token reduction is further limited by the requirement to reconstruct dense, pixel-aligned features, and the overhead of computing merge maps can negate expected performance improvements on modern accelerators. We introduce Mutual Pair Merging (MPM), a training-free token aggregation module that forms mutual nearest-neighbor pairs in cosine space, averages each pair, and records a merge map enabling a gather-based reconstruction before the decoder. This allows existing segmentation heads to remain unchanged. MPM does not introduce any learned parameters or continuous compression knobs. The trade-off between speed and accuracy is determined by a discrete insertion schedule. Benchmarked on an NVIDIA H100 GPU (with and without FlashAttention-2) and a Raspberry Pi 5 across standard segmentation datasets, MPM reduces per-image latency by up to 60% for ViT-Tiny on Raspberry Pi 5, and increases throughput by up to 20% on H100 with FlashAttention-2 while maintaining the mIoU drop below 3%.

Subjects: Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV)

Comments: Accepted to CVPR 2026 (Findings)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05718v1
**Subjects:** Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

**Abstract:** Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant potential for advancing scientific discovery. However, their ability to innovate fundamentally remains uncertain. This study investigates whether LLMs can reinvent foundational algorithms in computer science. The researchers developed an "Unlearn-and-Reinvent" pipeline which involves removing specific foundational algorithms from the LLM's pre-trained knowledge and then testing if the model can recreate these algorithms under controlled conditions. Using a GRPO-based, on-policy unlearning method, the team conducted experiments across ten target algorithms, three strong open-weight models, and three different levels of hints. Results indicate that the strongest model, Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507, successfully reinvented 50%, 70%, and 90% of the algorithms without hints, at hint level 1, and at hint level 2 respectively. Additionally, the study found that while high-level hints improve reinvention success rates, detailed step-by-step hints do not always help with complex algorithms. Furthermore, test-time reinforcement learning was effective in enabling the reinvention of the Strassen algorithm at hint level 2. Analysis of the output trajectories and ablation studies revealed that the generative verifier during the reinvention phase is crucial for maintaining the model’s reasoning strength and preventing the "thought collapse" phenomenon. These findings provide insights into both the potential and limitations of LLMs in innovative thinking.

Title: Let Geometry GUIDE: Layer-wise Unrolling of Geometric Priors in Multimodal LLMs

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown significant progress in 2D visual tasks but lack sufficient physical spatial awareness when dealing with real-world visual streams. The paper introduces GUIDE (Geometric Unrolling Inside MLLM Early-layers), a framework designed to inject geometric priors progressively into MLLMs. GUIDE captures multi-granularity geometric features through multi-level sampling within the geometric encoder and fuses these features with the early layers of MLLMs. It also includes a context-aware gating mechanism to enhance the utilization of spatial priors and suppress redundant geometric noise. Title: LUDOBENCH: Evaluating LLM Behavioural Decision-Making Through Spot-Based Board Game Scenarios in Ludo

Abstract: We introduce LudoBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate Large Language Model (LLM) strategic reasoning in the game of Ludo. This stochastic multi-agent board game includes dice mechanics, piece capture, safe-square navigation, and home-path progression, which introduce significant planning complexity. LudoBench consists of 480 handcrafted scenarios across 12 distinct decision categories, each focusing on a specific strategic choice. Additionally, the authors provide a fully functional 4-player Ludo simulator that supports various types of agents including Random, Heuristic, Game-Theory, and LLM agents. The game-theory agent utilizes Expectiminimax search with depth-limited lookahead to establish a principled strategic ceiling beyond simple heuristic strategies. Evaluating six models from four different model families, the study finds that all models align with the game-theory baseline only 40-46% of the time. Behavioral analysis reveals two main archetypes among the models: "finishers" who focus on completing pieces without developing them, and "builders" who prioritize development over completion. Each archetype captures only half of the game theory strategy. Furthermore, the study observes measurable behavioral shifts in models when subjected to history-conditioned grudge framing on identical board states, highlighting prompt sensitivity as a critical vulnerability. LudoBench offers a lightweight and interpretable framework for assessing LLM strategic reasoning under uncertainty. All related code, the dataset (480 entries), and model outputs are accessible at the provided URL.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Computation and Language (cs.CL); Computer Science and Game Theory (cs.GT); Machine Learning (cs.LG); Multiagent Systems (cs.MA)

Title: CuraLight: Debate-Guided Data Curation for LLM-Centered Traffic Signal Control

Abstract: Traffic signal control (TSC) is a key part of intelligent transportation systems (ITS). Current methods using reinforcement learning (RL) and large language models (LLMs) improve adaptability but face challenges like limited interpretability and weak generalization to diverse intersections. 

This paper introduces CuraLight, a framework where an RL agent helps fine-tune an LLM-based traffic signal controller. The RL agent explores traffic environments and creates high-quality interaction data used for imitation learning. A multi-LLM ensemble evaluates potential signal timing changes through structured debates, offering preference-aware feedback for training.

Experiments in SUMO across real-world networks from Jinan, Hangzhou, and Yizhuang show CuraLight outperforms existing methods, reducing average travel time by 5.34%, average queue length by 5.14%, and average waiting time by 7.02%. These results emphasize the benefits of combining RL-assisted exploration with structured debate for traffic signal control.

Title:LLM Reasoning as Trajectories: Step-Specific Representation Geometry and Correctness Signals

Abstract:This work characterizes large language models' chain-of-thought generation as a structured trajectory through representation space. Mathematical reasoning traverses functionally ordered, step-specific subspaces becoming increasingly separable with layer depth. Early reasoning steps follow similar trajectories but diverge systematically at later stages. Late-stage divergence enables mid-reasoning prediction of final-answer correctness with ROC-AUC up to 0.87. The study introduces trajectory-based steering, an inference-time intervention framework enabling reasoning correction and length control based on derived ideal trajectories.

Title:PanopticQuery: Unified Query-Time Reasoning for 4D Scenes

Abstract: Understanding dynamic 4D environments through natural language queries requires accurate scene reconstruction and robust semantic grounding.
