Title: From Uniform to Learned Knots: A Study of Spline-Based Numerical Encodings for Tabular Deep Learning

Abstract: This study investigates the impact of spline-based numerical encodings on tabular deep learning tasks. The research evaluates three types of splines—B-splines, M-splines, and integrated splines (I-splines)—under various knot placement strategies including uniform, quantile-based, target-aware, and learnable knots. The learnable-knot variants utilize a differentiable knot parameterization for end-to-end optimization alongside the model backbone. Evaluations were conducted across multiple public datasets using MLP, ResNet, and FT-Transformer architectures. Results indicate that the effectiveness of numerical encodings varies significantly depending on the task type, output size, and model architecture. For classification tasks, piecewise-linear encoding (PLE) performs best overall, while spline-based methods remain competitive. In regression tasks, performance varies based on the spline type, knot placement method, and output size, with MLP and ResNet generally showing better performance compared to FT-Transformer. Additionally, learnable-knot variants offer stable optimization but increase training costs, particularly for M-splines and I-splines. The study emphasizes the need to consider both predictive accuracy and computational efficiency when selecting numerical encodings.

Abstract: Remote sensing imagery often faces issues such as clouds, haze, noise, resolution limits, and sensor heterogeneity. Current restoration and fusion methods typically require separate models for each type of degradation. This research introduces Language-conditioned Large-scale Remote Sensing restoration model (LLaRS), which is the first unified foundational model designed for multi-modal and multi-task remote sensing low-level vision tasks. Subjects: Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV), Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

**arXiv:2604.05613v1**

Abstract: Autoregressive graph generators define likelihoods through a sequential construction process. However, these likelihoods are only meaningful if they remain consistent across all linearizations of the same graph. The study introduces Segmented Eulerian Neighborhood Trails (SENT), a method that converts graphs into sequences decodable by language models. It highlights issues where multiple equivalent linearizations exist for the same graph, leading to inconsistencies in assigned negative log-likelihood (NLL).

The research quantifies these inconsistencies using the coefficient of variation across equivalent linearizations, termed Linearization Uncertainty (LU). Through experiments involving training transformers under four different linearization strategies on two datasets, it demonstrates that models trained with biased orderings achieve lower NLL on their native order but exhibit significantly higher Expected Calibration Error (ECE) under random permutations. This indicates that such models learn the specific training linearization rather than the inherent properties of the graph.

On the molecular graph benchmark QM9, the study finds that NLL for generated graphs correlates negatively with molecular stability (AUC = 0.43), whereas LU correlates positively with molecular stability (AUC = 0.85). This suggests that permutation-based evaluation offers a more reliable measure for assessing the quality of generated molecules.

Code for the research is available at [this link](https://this https URL).

Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Title: Label Effects: Shared Heuristic Reliance in Trust Assessment by Humans and LLM-as-a-Judge

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as automated evaluators (LLM-as-a-Judge). This study reveals that trust judgments made by LLMs can be biased by source labels. Both humans and LLMs assign higher trust to information labeled as human-authored compared to the same content labeled as AI-generated. Eye-tracking data shows humans rely heavily on source labels as heuristic cues. Analysis of LLM internal states indicates that models allocate more attention to the label region than the content region, with stronger bias towards human labels over AI labels. Decision uncertainty is also higher under AI labels. These findings suggest that source labels act as significant heuristic cues for both humans and LLMs, raising concerns about the validity of label-sensitive LLM evaluations and highlighting the need for unbiased evaluation methods.

**arXiv:2604.05558v1**

Abstract: The missing modality problem reduces model accuracy and generalization in real-world scenarios for multimodal sentiment analysis. Current methods focus on improving robustness through prompt learning and pre-trained models but face two main limitations: the lack of rigorous evaluation for generating missing modalities and insufficient exploration of structural dependencies among multimodal prompts and their global coherence. 

The authors propose a Prompt-based Missing Modality Adaptation framework. This includes:
- A Missing Modality Evaluator that uses pre-trained models and pseudo labels to assess the importance of missing modalities.
- A Modality-invariant Prompt Disentanglement module to capture intrinsic local correlations and improve representation quality.
- A Dynamic Prompt Weighting module to compute mutual information-based weights and suppress interference from missing modalities.
- A Multi-level Prompt Dynamic Connection module to integrate shared prompts with self-attention outputs, enhancing global consistency.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) face efficiency barriers due to visual token dominance, influenced by high-resolution feature extraction, quadratic attention scaling, and memory bandwidth constraints. Title: Referring-Aware Visuomotor Policy Learning for Closed-Loop Manipulation

Abstract: This paper introduces the Referring-Aware Visuomotor Policy (ReV), a closed-loop framework designed to enhance the robustness of robotic manipulation in out-of-distribution scenarios. The framework adapts to unforeseen circumstances by incorporating sparse referring points from humans or high-level planners. It uses coupled diffusion heads to maintain standard task execution patterns while integrating sparse references through a trajectory-steering strategy. Training involves applying targeted perturbations to expert demonstrations without needing additional data or fine-tuning, achieving higher success rates in both simulated and real-world tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can manage outdated information effectively through knowledge editing. This research identifies two primary limitations in current approaches: poor generalization due to rigid injection of new knowledge without ensuring effective usage, and a narrow scope focusing mainly on structured fact triples while ignoring diverse unstructured factual information. To tackle these issues, the authors propose a new paradigm called "Chain of Thoughts (CoTs) reasoning for knowledge editing (CoT2Edit)". This involves using language model agents to generate CoTs from both structured and unstructured edited data, creating high-quality instructional datasets. The model is then fine-tuned using supervised learning and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). During inference, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is used to dynamically retrieve relevant edited facts for real-time knowledge editing. Experiments show strong generalization across six diverse scenarios with a single round of training on three open-source language models.

Title: Turbulence-like 5/3 spectral scaling in contextual representations of language as a complex system

Abstract: Natural language is represented as a trajectory in a high-dimensional embedding space generated by transformer-based models. The study quantifies scale-dependent fluctuations in text sequences and finds a robust power law with an exponent close to $5/3$ in the power spectrum across multiple languages and corpora. This scaling is observed in contextual embeddings from both human-written and AI-generated text but not in static word embeddings or randomized text. The findings suggest that semantic information is integrated in a scale-free, self-similar manner across linguistic scales, providing a benchmark for studying complex structures in language representations.

Title: SignalClaw: LLM-Guided Evolutionary Synthesis of Interpretable Traffic Signal Control Skills

Abstract: Accurate segmentation of 3D medical scans is essential for clinical diagnostics and treatment planning. Existing methods often struggle to balance high accuracy and computational efficiency across various anatomies and imaging modalities. To tackle these issues, the authors introduce GCNV-Net, a novel 3D medical segmentation framework. This framework includes a Tri-directional Dynamic Nonvoid Voxel Transformer (3DNVT), a Geometrical Cross-Attention module (GCA), and Nonvoid Voxelization. The 3DNVT partitions relevant voxels along three orthogonal anatomical planes, facilitating the modeling of complex 3D spatial dependencies. The GCA enhances fine-grained anatomical segmentation accuracy by incorporating geometric positional information during multi-scale feature fusion. Nonvoid Voxelization reduces redundant computation by processing only informative regions, achieving a 56.13% reduction in FLOPs and a 68.49% reduction in inference latency compared to traditional voxelization.

Evaluation: GCNV-Net was evaluated on several widely recognized benchmarks including BraTS2021, ACDC, MSD Prostate, MSD Pancreas, and AMOS2022. It achieved state-of-the-art performance, surpassing current methods by 0.65% on Dice, 0.63% on IoU, 1% on NSD, and 14.5% on HD95.

Abstract: Protecting the intellectual property of open-weight large language models (LLMs) requires verifying whether a suspect model is derived from a victim model despite common laundering operations such as fine-tuning (including PPO/DPO), pruning/compression, and model merging. Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) are becoming promising alternatives to autoregressive (AR) LLMs. When applied to multimodal tasks, they form diffusion multimodal large language models (dMLLMs), which are expected to maintain reasoning capabilities while offering faster inference through parallel generation. **arXiv:2604.05469v1** (stat)

This paper, titled "Task Ecologies and the Evolution of World-Tracking Representations in Large Language Models," studies large language models as evolving organisms. It explores conditions under which autoregressive next-token learning selects for world-tracking representations. Key findings include:
- The Bayes-optimal next-token cross-entropy decomposes into irreducible conditional entropy and a Jensen-Shannon excess term.
- The excess term vanishes if the encoding preserves the training ecology's equivalence classes.
- Frozen dense and frozen Mixture-of-Experts transformers satisfy the fixed-encoding analysis, while in-context learning and per-task adaptation do not.
- Two characteristic failure modes are identified: simplicity pressure removing low-gain distinctions and positive excess on deployment ecologies refining the training ecology.
- Controlled experiments validate theoretical predictions on representational selection in small language models.

Subjects: Methodology (stat.ME), Machine Learning (cs.LG), Machine Learning (stat.ML)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05469v1
**arXiv:2604.05467v1** introduces CUE-R, a framework for measuring the operational utility of individual evidence items in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The study evaluates the impact of perturbations (REMOVE, REPLACE, DUPLICATE) on evidence items and measures changes across three utility axes: correctness, grounding faithfulness, and confidence error. Experiments conducted on datasets like HotpotQA and 2WikiMultihopQA indicate that removing or replacing evidence significantly affects correctness and grounding, while duplicating evidence often leads to redundant answers without altering behavior significantly. The research highlights the importance of evaluating evidence beyond just the final answer, suggesting that current evaluation methods may overlook critical evidence effects.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05467v1
Hierarchical Contrastive Learning (HCL) is proposed as a framework for learning globally shared, partially shared, and modality-specific representations in multimodal data. The method uses a hierarchical latent-variable formulation combined with structural sparsity and a structure-aware contrastive objective. This approach ensures alignment only between modalities that genuinely share a latent factor. The study proves the identifiability of the hierarchical decomposition under uncorrelated latent variables and establishes recovery guarantees for loading matrices. It also derives parameter estimation and excess-risk bounds for downstream predictions. Simulations demonstrate accurate recovery of hierarchical structures and effective selection of task-relevant components. In practical applications, HCL has yielded more informative representations and improved predictive performance on multimodal electronic health records.

Title:LLM Evaluation as Tensor Completion: Low Rank Structure and Semiparametric Efficiency

**arXiv:2604.05446v1** (stat)

Abstract: Obtaining high-quality labels is costly, whereas unlabeled covariates are often abundant, motivating semi-supervised inference methods with reliable uncertainty quantification. Prediction-powered inference (PPI) leverages a machine-learning predictor trained on a small labeled sample to improve efficiency, but it can lose efficiency under model misspecification and suffer from coverage distortions due to label reuse. We introduce Machine-Learning-Assisted Generalized Entropy Calibration (MEC), a cross-fitted, calibration-weighted variant of PPI. MEC improves efficiency by reweighting labeled samples to better align with the target population, using a principled calibration framework based on Bregman projections. This yields robustness to affine transformations of the predictor and relaxes requirements for validity by replacing conditions on raw prediction error with weaker projection-error conditions. As a result, MEC attains the semiparametric efficiency bound under weaker assumptions than existing PPI variants. Across simulations and a real-data application, MEC achieves near-nominal coverage and tighter confidence intervals than CF-PPI and vanilla PPI.

Subjects: Machine Learning (stat.ML); Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Cite as: arXiv:2604.05446 [stat.ML]

Title: LanG -- A Governance-Aware Agentic AI Platform for Unified Security Operations

Abstract: Modern Security Operations Centers face challenges such as alert fatigue, fragmented tooling, and limited cross-source event correlation. Current Security Information Event Management and Extended Detection and Response systems only partially address these issues. The paper introduces LanG, an open-source, governance-aware agentic AI platform designed to unify security operations. Key contributions include:
(i) A Unified Incident Context Record with a correlation engine achieving an F1 score of 87%.
(ii) An Agentic AI Orchestrator on LangGraph with human-in-the-loop checkpoints.
(iii) An LLM-based Rule Generator fine-tuned on four base models, producing deployable Snort 2/3, Suricata, and YARA rules with an average acceptance rate of 96.2%.
(iv) A Three-Phase Attack Reconstructor using Louvain community detection, LLM-driven hypothesis generation, and Bayesian scoring, achieving 87.5% kill-chain accuracy.
(v) A layered Governance-MCP-Agentic AI-Security architecture where all tools are exposed via the Model Context Protocol, governed by an AI Governance Policy Engine with a two-layer guardrail pipeline (regex + Llama Prompt Guard 2 semantic classifier, achieving 98.1% F1 score with experimental zero false positives).

The platform supports multi-tenant isolation, role-based access, and fully local deployment. It achieves weighted F1 scores of 99.0% and 91.0% for anomaly and threat detection, respectively, in intrusion-detection benchmarks. Inferences run in approximately 21 ms, with a machine-side mean time to detect of 1.58 seconds. The rule generator exceeds 91% deployability on live IDS engines. A systematic comparison against eight SOC platforms confirms that LanG uniquely satisfies multiple industrial capabilities in one open-source tool while enforcing selected AI governance policies.

Title: Your LLM Agent Can Leak Your Data: Data Exfiltration via Backdoored Tool Use

Abstract: This paper introduces Back-Reveal, a data exfiltration attack that embeds semantic triggers into fine-tuned large language model (LLM) agents. When triggered, the backdoored agent retrieves stored user context through memory-access tool calls and leaks it via disguised retrieval tool calls. Multi-turn interactions amplify the impact, allowing sustained and cumulative information leakage over time. Experimental results highlight a critical vulnerability in LLM agents with tool access and emphasize the need for defenses against such attacks.

Title: ALTO: Adaptive LoRA Tuning and Orchestration for Heterogeneous LoRA Training Workloads

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is the leading method for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models. However, achieving high-quality adapters often necessitates systematic hyperparameter tuning due to LoRA’s sensitivity to configuration choices. This results in numerous concurrent LoRA jobs across heterogeneous tasks in multi-tenant environments. Current systems typically manage these jobs independently, leading to wasted computational resources and underutilized GPUs. 

The paper introduces ALTO (Adaptive LoRA Tuning and Orchestration), a co-designed training system aimed at accelerating LoRA hyperparameter tuning and facilitating efficient cluster sharing across diverse tasks. Key insights include monitoring loss trajectories to terminate unpromising configurations early, utilizing fused grouped GEMM along with a novel rank-local adapter parallelism to optimize GPU usage, and integrating intra-task and inter-task scheduling to enhance multi-task placement efficiency. Evaluations demonstrate that ALTO can achieve up to 13.8 times speedup compared to existing methods without compromising adapter quality.

Abstract: The paper introduces PRISM-MCTS, a novel reasoning framework inspired by human parallel thinking and reflective processes. It addresses the inefficiencies of traditional Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) methods by integrating a Process Reward Model (PRM) with a dynamic shared memory. This approach captures both heuristic and fallacy aspects of reasoning trajectories, allowing for the reinforcement of successful strategies and the pruning of error-prone branches. The authors also present a data-efficient training strategy for the PRM, which achieves high-fidelity evaluation under a few-shot regime. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that PRISM-MCTS halves the trajectory requirements on GPQA and outperforms MCTS-RAG and Search-o1, indicating its ability to scale inference efficiently through reasoned decision-making rather than exhaustive search.

Title: Reason Analogically via Cross-domain Prior Knowledge: An Empirical Study of Cross-domain Knowledge Transfer for In-Context Learning

Abstract: Existing in-context learning (ICL) depends on in-domain expert demonstrations, which limits its applicability when such annotations are scarce. The study proposes that different domains might share underlying reasoning structures, allowing demonstrations from one domain to enhance inference in another despite semantic differences. Researchers conducted an empirical study using various retrieval methods to validate the feasibility of transferring knowledge across domains within the ICL framework. Results indicate conditional positive transfer in cross-domain ICL, identifying a threshold where additional demonstrations lead to greater improvements. These gains are attributed to the repair of reasoning structures through cross-domain examples rather than semantic cues. The study concludes by validating the potential of leveraging cross-domain knowledge transfer to enhance ICL performance, encouraging further exploration into more effective retrieval methods.

**Title:** LMI-Net: Linear Matrix Inequality--Constrained Neural Networks via Differentiable Projection Layers

**Abstract:** Linear matrix inequalities (LMIs) play a crucial role in certifying stability, robustness, and forward invariance of dynamical systems. The proposed LMI-Net introduces an efficient and modular differentiable projection layer that enforces LMI constraints. This method converts the set defined by LMIs into an intersection of an affine equality constraint and the positive semidefinite cone, facilitating forward passes through Douglas-Rachford splitting and supporting efficient backward propagation via implicit differentiation. Theoretical guarantees confirm the convergence of the projection layer to a feasible point, ensuring that LMI-Net transforms generic neural networks into reliable models adhering to LMI constraints. Experiments show substantial improvements in feasibility over soft-constrained models under distribution shifts while maintaining fast inference speeds.

Title: 3DTurboQuant: Training-Free Near-Optimal Quantization for 3D Reconstruction Models

Abstract: Existing methods for compressing 3D Gaussian Splatting, NeRF, or transformer-based 3D reconstructors require learning a data-dependent codebook through per-scene fine-tuning. This paper demonstrates that such learning is unnecessary. It introduces a precomputed, data-independent Lloyd-Max quantization method that achieves near-optimal results within a factor of 2.7 of the information-theoretic lower bound. Key contributions include:
- A dimension-dependent criterion predicting quantizable parameters and their bit-widths.
- Norm-separation bounds linking quantization mean squared error (MSE) to rendering peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR).
- An entry-grouping strategy extending rotation-based quantization to 2D hash grid features.
- A composable pruning-quantization pipeline with a closed-form compression ratio.

Performance highlights:
- Compresses 3DGS by 3.5x with 0.02dB PSNR loss.
- Compresses DUSt3R KV caches by 7.9x with 39.7dB pointmap fidelity.

The method requires no training, codebook learning, or calibration data, and compression takes only seconds.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05366v1
TFRBench: A Reasoning Benchmark for Evaluating Forecasting Systems introduces the first benchmark designed to assess the reasoning capabilities of forecasting systems. Unlike traditional evaluations which focus solely on numerical accuracy, TFRBench evaluates how well forecasting systems can analyze cross-channel dependencies, trends, and external events. The benchmark uses a systematic multi-agent framework with an iterative verification loop to generate numerically grounded reasoning traces. Evaluation across ten datasets in five domains shows that the reasoning produced by TFRBench is causally effective and improves forecasting accuracy when used to prompt large language models (LLMs). Off-the-shelf LLMs, however, struggle with both reasoning and numerical forecasting tasks, often failing to capture domain-specific dynamics accurately. This benchmark sets a new standard for interpretable, reasoning-based evaluation in time-series forecasting.

Rethinking IRSTD: Single-Point Supervision Guided Encoder-only Framework is Enough for Infrared Small Target Detection

Abstract: This paper argues that traditional "encoder-decoder" segmentation paradigms used in Infrared Small Target Detection (IRSTD) often overlook the challenge of detecting small targets that occupy only a few pixels and are typically blurred by cluttered backgrounds. The authors propose a new approach called SPIRE (Single-Point Supervision guided Infrared Probabilistic Response Encoding), which focuses on target localization rather than complete separation of the target region from the background. SPIRE uses a Point-Response Prior Supervision (PRPS) method to transform single-point annotations into probabilistic response maps, enabling an encoder-only framework for end-to-end regression without the need for a decoder. Experiments show that SPIRE achieves competitive detection performance with low false alarm rates and reduced computational costs compared to existing methods.

Title:ETR: Entropy Trend Reward for Efficient Chain-of-Thought Reasoning

Abstract:Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning enhances large language model performance on complex tasks but often results in overly long and inefficient reasoning paths. Current methods reduce CoT lengths through penalties on length or global entropy reduction, assuming lower uncertainty is always beneficial. The authors argue that reasoning efficiency is better understood by the trend of uncertainty rather than its absolute level. They introduce Entropy Trend Reward (ETR), which promotes progressive uncertainty reduction while permitting some local exploration. Evaluations across various reasoning models and benchmarks demonstrate that ETR significantly improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off, achieving a 9.9% increase in accuracy and a 67% reduction in CoT length compared to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B.

**Abstract:** The paper discusses an unsupervised multi-agent and single-agent perception framework called UMS, which leverages multi-agent cooperation to improve 3D object detection performance. This system enhances Image Goal Navigation (ImageNav) by treating the goal image as a geometric query, allowing for precise positioning beyond the standard 1-meter success criterion. Abstract: Hallucinations in medical large language models (LLMs) remain a critical safety issue, especially when there is insufficient or conflicting evidence. The research focuses on diabetic retinopathy (DR) decision-making scenarios and introduces RETINA-SAFE, a benchmark based on retinal grading records, consisting of 12,522 samples. RETINA-SAFE includes three types of tasks: E-Align (consistent with evidence), E-Conflict (conflicting with evidence), and E-Gap (insufficient evidence).

The study also proposes ECRT (Evidence-Conditioned Risk Triage), a two-stage detection framework. The first stage performs safe/unsafe risk classification, while the second stage refines unsafe cases into contradictions and evidence gaps. ECRT uses internal representations and logit shifts under CTX/NOCTX conditions, with balanced training for robust learning. It shows improved performance compared to external uncertainty and self-consistency baselines, as well as the strongest adapted supervised baseline, providing better balanced accuracy in risk triage.

Title: TRACE: Capability-Targeted Agentic Training

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) deployed in agentic environments must exercise multiple capabilities across different task instances. The proposed method, TRACE (Turning Recurrent Agent failures into Capability-targeted training Environments), identifies lacking capabilities by contrasting successful and failed trajectories, synthesizes targeted training environments, and trains a LoRA adapter via reinforcement learning (RL). Graph of Skills (GoS) is introduced as an inference-time structural retrieval layer designed for large skill libraries in modern agent systems. The method constructs an executable skill graph offline from skill packages and retrieves a bounded, dependency-aware skill bundle during inference using hybrid semantic-lexical seeding, reverse-weighted Personalized PageRank, and context-budgeted hydration. This approach improves the average reward by 43.6% compared to the baseline of loading the full skill set, while reducing input tokens by 37.8%. It also generalizes across three model families: Claude Sonnet, GPT-5.2 Codex, and MiniMax. Ablation studies show consistent performance improvements over both vanilla skills loading and simple vector retrieval methods in balancing reward, token efficiency, and runtime.

Abstract:Harmful content detectors, particularly disinformation classifiers, are primarily developed and tested using Standard American English (SAE). This leaves their effectiveness against dialectal variations untested. The study introduces DIA-HARM, a benchmark for assessing disinformation detection robustness across 50 English dialects. Using Multi-VALUE's linguistic transformations, the researchers created D3, a corpus of 195K samples derived from existing disinformation datasets. Evaluating 16 detection models revealed significant vulnerabilities when faced with human-written dialectal content, leading to a decrease in detection accuracy by 1.4-3.6% F1 score. In contrast, AI-generated content showed stability. Fine-tuned transformers performed better than zero-shot large language models (LLMs), achieving up to 96.6% F1 score compared to 78.3% for the latter. Some models experienced catastrophic failures with over 33% degradation on mixed content. Multilingual models generalized well across dialects, whereas monolingual models struggled. The research highlights that current disinformation detectors may disadvantage non-SAE speakers globally. The DIA-HARM framework, D3 corpus, and evaluation tools are available at this https URL.

**arXiv:2604.05306v1**

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being used in scenarios where decision-making relies on uncertainty, such as abstaining from action, retrieving information, or verifying facts. Current methods typically estimate uncertainty after generating output, treating it as a hidden variable. This paper proposes studying uncertainty as a control interface. Two approaches are explored: a global interface where the model expresses a calibrated confidence score for its final response, and a local interface where the model indicates uncertainty during reasoning processes. The global approach enhances calibration and reduces overconfidence errors, leading to better performance in adaptive RAG controllers. The local approach highlights silent failures and triggers high-recall retrievals effectively. Internally, these methods operate differently; the global method refines how uncertainty is interpreted, while the local method causes broader changes in later layers of the model. The research suggests that effective uncertainty expression in LLMs should be tailored to specific tasks, with global confidence for final answers and local signals for identifying necessary interventions.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05306v1
**arXiv:2604.05302v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: Text simplification aids in second language (L2) learning by offering understandable content, aligning with the Input Hypothesis. The creation of personalized parallel corpora is expensive, and current large language model (LLM)-based readability control methods depend on pre-labeled sentence corpora and mainly focus on English. The authors introduce Re-RIGHT, a unified reinforcement learning framework designed for adaptive multilingual text simplification without needing parallel corpus supervision. They demonstrate that prompting-based lexical simplification at targeted proficiency levels (such as CEFR, JLPT, TOPIK, and HSK) performs poorly at simpler levels and for non-English languages, even when using advanced models like GPT-5.2 and Gemini 2.5. To improve this, they collected 43K vocabulary-level data across four languages (English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese) and trained a compact 4B policy model using Re-RIGHT. This model incorporates three reward modules: vocabulary coverage, semantic preservation, and coherence. Compared to stronger LLM baselines, Re-RIGHT achieves better lexical coverage at targeted proficiency levels while preserving the original meaning and fluency.

**Subjects:** Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

**Abstract:** Value factorization in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) often converges to suboptimal stable points due to theoretical and algorithmic limitations. The paper introduces a novel theoretical concept, the stable point, which helps characterize the convergence issues of value factorization. It identifies non-optimal stable points as the main reason for poor performance. The authors propose a Multi-Round Value Factorization (MRVF) framework that iteratively filters out suboptimal actions, transforming them into unstable points to drive the system towards optimal solutions. Experiments on challenging benchmarks like predator-prey tasks and StarCraft II Multi-Agent Challenge confirm the effectiveness of MRVF compared to existing methods.

- **タイトル**: Broken by Default: A Formal Verification Study of Security Vulnerabilities in AI-Generated Code
- **著者**: Dominik Blain, Maxime Noiseux
- **概要**: AIコード生成アシスタントがセキュリティに敏感な領域で使用されているが、その出力の脆弱性は未評価である。この研究では、7つの先進的なLLMによって生成された3,500のコードアーティファクトを500のセキュリティクリティカルなプロンプト（5つのCWEカテゴリ、各カテゴリ100プロンプト）に対してZ3 SMTソルバーを使用して形式的に検証した。結果、55.8%のアーティファクトが少なくとも1つ以上の脆弱性を持っていることが確認され、そのうち1,055個はZ3により形式的に証明された。GPT-4oは最悪の成績を収め（62.4%，F評価）、Gemini 2.5 Flashが最も良い成績を収めた（48.4%, D評価）。実行時クラッシュを引き起こす脆弱性は6つのモデルで確認された。また、明示的なセキュリティ指示は平均的な脆弱性率を4ポイントしか低下させないことが判明した。さらに、6つの業界ツールの組み合わせでも97.8%のZ3証明された脆弱性を見逃していることが明らかになった。

\[Submitted on 7 Apr 2026\]

Title: Pressure, What Pressure? Sycophancy Disentanglement in Language Models via Reward Decomposition

Abstract: Large language models exhibit sycophancy, which is the tendency to align their responses with perceived user preferences or authority cues irrespective of evidence. Standard alignment methods fail to address this issue because they combine two distinct failure modes—pressure capitulation and evidence blindness—into a single reward signal. The authors introduce a method called Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO), which decomposes the reward signal into five components: pressure resistance, context fidelity, position consistency, agreement suppression, and factual correctness. This approach aims to reduce sycophancy in language models.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

Authors: Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Ahsan Bilal, Muhammad Umer, Emily Fox

Title: Spec Kit Agents: Context-Grounded Agentic Workflows

Abstract: Spec-driven development (SDD) using AI coding agents can provide a structured workflow. **arXiv:2604.05248v1**

Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG); Computation and Language (cs.CL)

Abstract: This paper introduces Sparse Memory Finetuning (SMF) as an alternative method to continually adapt Large Language Models (LLMs) to new knowledge without degrading their existing capabilities. SMF localizes updates to a small subset of parameters in explicit memory layers, avoiding the issue of catastrophic forgetting seen in standard finetuning methods. The authors propose an open-source pipeline to integrate sparse memory modules into existing pretrained models, specifically Qwen-2.5-0.5B, facilitating effective continual learning on consumer hardware. A novel slot-selection mechanism based on Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence is introduced to prioritize memory updates for "surprising" tokens relative to a background distribution. Experimental results show that the retrofitted models can acquire new factual knowledge while minimizing forgetting of previously learned information.

Useful Information Summary:
- Focuses on Sparse Memory Finetuning (SMF) for continual learning.
- Proposes an open-source pipeline for integrating sparse memory modules into LLMs.
- Introduces a slot-selection mechanism using KL divergence to prioritize memory updates.
- Demonstrates minimal forgetting of previous knowledge when acquiring new facts.

Title: XMark: Reliable Multi-Bit Watermarking for LLM-Generated Texts

Abstract: Multi-bit watermarking is presented as a solution for embedding imperceptible binary messages into Large Language Model (LLM)-generated text. This technique enables reliable attribution and tracing of malicious usage of LLMs. Current methods face limitations such as computational infeasibility for large messages and poor trade-offs between text quality and decoding accuracy. The proposed method, **XMark**, addresses these issues by producing less distorted logit distributions for watermarked token generation, preserving text quality, and enabling reliable message recovery even with limited tokens. Experiments demonstrate significant improvements in decoding accuracy while maintaining text quality, surpassing previous methods.

**arXiv:2604.05217v1** (cs)

Abstract: Neural language models are insensitive to word order, so positional encodings are used to address this issue. This paper establishes a mathematical theory for positional encodings, proving four key results:
1. Any Transformer without a positional signal cannot solve tasks dependent on word order.
2. Training assigns unique vector representations to different sequence positions at global minima.
3. An optimal approximation to an information-optimal encoding is constructed using classical multidimensional scaling (MDS).
4. The optimal encoding has an effective rank and can be represented with fewer parameters compared to traditional methods.

Appendix A provides proofs related to the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regime for various loss functions, confirming theoretical predictions through experiments on SST-2 and IMDB datasets.

Abstract: Representation learning on large-scale unstructured volumetric and surface meshes presents significant challenges in neuroimaging, particularly when integrating diverse vertex-level morphometric descriptors like cortical thickness, curvature, sulcal depth, and myelin content. The proposed method introduces a hierarchical transformer framework that operates on spatially adaptive tree partitions derived from simplicial complexes. This framework supports both volumetric and surface discretizations within a unified architecture, facilitating efficient multi-scale attention without requiring topology-specific adjustments. **arXiv:2604.05212v1** (cs)

Abstract: Detecting and localizing objects in space is a fundamental computer vision problem. The proposed algorithm, Boxer, estimates static 3D bounding boxes (3DBBs) from 2D open-vocabulary object detections, posed images, and optional depth data. BoxerNet, a transformer-based network, lifts 2D bounding box (2DBB) proposals into 3D space. It uses multi-view fusion and geometric filtering to produce globally consistent de-duplicated 3DBBs. Boxer leverages existing 2DBB detection algorithms to reduce the need for costly annotated 3DBB training data. It incorporates aleatoric uncertainty for robust regression, median depth patch encoding for sparse depth inputs, and trains with over 1.2 million unique 3DBBs. BoxerNet outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in open-world 3DBB lifting tasks.

Integration of Object Detection and Small VLMs for Construction Safety Hazard Identification

Abstract: This study addresses the need for accurate and timely identification of construction hazards to prevent workplace accidents. It compares large vision-language models (VLMs) with small vision-language models (sVLMs), noting that while large VLMs excel in contextual reasoning, they are not suitable for real-time applications due to high computational demands. Small VLMs, though more efficient, often lack precision and reliability in complex scenarios.

The research proposes a detection-guided sVLM framework that combines object detection with multimodal reasoning to enhance hazard identification. Using a YOLOv11n detector, it localizes workers and machinery before guiding sVLMs through structured prompts for better hazard assessment. Six sVLMs were tested on a dataset of annotated construction site images, showing consistent improvements in hazard detection performance. The best model achieved an F1-score of 50.6%, a significant improvement over the baseline's 34.5%. Additionally, explanation quality improved from BERTScore F1 of 0.61 to 0.82.

The integration method adds minimal overhead, increasing inference time by only 2.5 ms per image. This demonstrates the effectiveness and efficiency of combining lightweight object detection with small VLMs for context-aware construction safety hazard detection.

**arXiv:2604.05183v1**

Abstract: This paper addresses the challenge of combining multiple adapters trained for different tasks into a single adapter capable of yielding satisfactory results across all tasks. Specifically, it focuses on merging subject and style adapters for generative models. The authors propose using structured orthogonal parametrization and its geometric properties to achieve training-free adapter merging in cases of orthogonal fine-tuning (OFT). They derive formulas for approximating geodesics between points and introduce a spectra restoration transform to enhance the quality of the fusion. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of their method in uniting concept and style features from different adapters. This is reportedly the first training-free approach for merging multiplicative orthogonal adapters.

Large Sparse Reconstruction Model (LSRM) is introduced to enhance feed-forward 3D reconstruction by scaling transformer context windows. Title: General Multimodal Protein Design Enables DNA-Encoding of Chemistry

Abstract: Evolution provides a wide range of enzymatic diversity, but the chemical space it explores is limited. Current deep generative models can design proteins that bind ligands, but they cannot create enzymes without specifying catalytic residues in advance. The researchers introduced DISCO (DIffusion for Sequence-structure CO-design), a multimodal model capable of designing protein sequences and 3D structures around any biomolecule. DISCO optimizes objectives across both modalities using inference-time scaling methods. It designs diverse heme enzymes based solely on reactive intermediates, enabling new-to-nature carbene-transfer reactions such as alkene cyclopropanation, spirocyclopropanation, B-H, and C(sp$^3$)-H insertions with high activities surpassing those of engineered enzymes. Directed evolution further improves enzyme activity. DISCO offers a scalable approach to creating evolvable enzymes, expanding the scope of genetically encodable transformations.

Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05181v1
Gradient-Controlled Decoding: A Safety Guardrail for LLMs with Dual-Anchor Steering is a paper submitted on April 6, 2026, by Purva Chiniya, Kevin Scaria, and Sagar Chaturvedi. The paper introduces Gradient-Controlled Decoding (GCD), a method that enhances the security of large language models (LLMs) against jailbreak and prompt-injection attacks without requiring additional training. GCD uses both an acceptance anchor token ("Sure") and a refusal anchor token ("Sorry") to tighten the decision boundary, thereby reducing false positives. When a prompt is flagged, GCD injects refusal tokens before resuming autoregressive decoding, ensuring the first token emitted is safe. This approach reduces false positives by 52% compared to GradSafe while maintaining similar recall rates. It also decreases the attack success rate by up to 10% relative to the strongest decoding-only baseline. The method adds less than 15-20 ms latency on average on V100 instances and can be transferred to various LLMs including LLaMA-2-7B, Mixtral-8x7B, and Qwen-2-7B. Only 20 demonstration templates are required for implementation.

It assesses 44 structured tasks across various scenarios, including single-service, cross-service, and safety-critical situations. Key findings indicate that fully scaffolded agents achieve task success rates ranging from 39% to 64%, though they also exhibit unsafe actions at rates between 7% and 33%.

The research identifies eight common patterns of unsafe behavior, such as multi-step sandbox escalation and silent contract modification.

**Abstract:** Reconfigurable Intelligent Surfaces (RIS) can create smart radio environments for next-generation millimeter-wave (mmWave) networks. **arXiv:2604.05164v1**

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) reasoning performance plateaus, improving inference-time compute efficiency is crucial to mitigate overthinking and long thinking traces even for simple queries. This work formulates multi-turn reasoning as a sequential compute allocation problem modeled as a multi-objective Markov Decision Process. The authors propose Turn-Adaptive Budgets (TAB), a budget allocation policy trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). TAB learns to maximize task accuracy while respecting global per-problem token constraints. It adapts to allocate smaller budgets to easier turns and saves appropriate tokens for harder reasoning steps. Experiments show TAB saves up to 35% tokens while maintaining accuracy compared to static and off-the-shelf LLM budget baselines. Additionally, for systems where a plan of all sub-questions is available beforehand, the authors propose TAB All-SubQ, which budgets tokens based on conversation history and all past and future sub-questions, saving up to 40% tokens over baselines.

Title: Bypassing the CSI Bottleneck: MARL-Driven Spatial Control for Reflector Arrays

Abstract: Reconfigurable Intelligent Surfaces (RIS) are crucial for next-generation smart radio environments but face significant bottlenecks due to the computational overhead of Channel State Information (CSI) estimation. The paper proposes an AI-driven approach using Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) to control mechanically adjustable metallic reflector arrays. This method reduces complexity by focusing on spatial intelligence rather than detailed channel modeling. The approach also shows resilience to localization noise, maintaining stable signal coverage even under 1-meter noise conditions.

Abstract: The research introduces "CovQValue," a method for LLM-based test generation that uses principles of Bayesian exploration to enhance branch coverage in software testing. This approach treats the program's branch structure as an unknown environment and evolves a coverage map as a probabilistic posterior. By feeding this map back into the LLM, it generates diverse candidate plans and selects the most informative one based on estimated Q-values. Title: IntentScore: Intent-Conditioned Action Evaluation for Computer-Use Agents

Abstract: Computer-Use Agents (CUAs) use large language models to perform GUI operations but often generate actions without assessing their quality, leading to cascading errors. The paper introduces IntentScore, a reward model that evaluates the quality of candidate actions based on planning intent. It was trained using 398K offline GUI interaction steps across three operating systems. IntentScore uses two objectives: contrastive alignment for state-action relevance and margin ranking for action correctness. It achieved 97.5% pairwise discrimination accuracy. When deployed as a re-ranker for Agent S3 on OSWorld, it improved the task success rate by 6.9 points, showing its ability to generalize to unseen agents and tasks.

Title:EffiPair: Improving the Efficiency of LLM-generated Code with Relative Contrastive Feedback

Abstract:Large language models (LLMs) often generate code that is functionally correct but inefficient in terms of runtime and memory. The proposed solution, Relative Contrastive Feedback (RCF), compares two similar programs for the same task and highlights differences related to efficiency. EffiPair, an iterative refinement framework, generates multiple candidate solutions, identifies efficient program pairs, summarizes their differences into feedback, and uses this feedback to improve efficiency. EffiPair achieves up to 1.5x speedup over generated code without performance feedback and reduces token usage by more than 90% compared to previous methods.

Abstract: Introduces SenseAI, a human-in-the-loop (HITL) validated financial sentiment dataset designed to capture model outputs along with their reasoning processes. This dataset includes reasoning chains, confidence scores, human corrections, and real-world market outcomes, aligning with Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) paradigms.

Implications: Suggests that LLM errors in financial reasoning can be systematically addressed through structured HITL data, aiding in targeted model improvements and financial AI system evaluations.

Title: Reasoning Through Chess: How Reasoning Evolves from Data Through Fine-Tuning and Reinforcement Learning

Abstract: This study explores how reasoning evolves in a language model through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). The research uses theoretically inspired datasets to analyze the impact on language model performance in chess. Key findings include:
- Direct prediction of the best move during fine-tuning leads to effective RL but results in unfaithful reasoning.
- Training on multi-move trajectories produces comparable performance with more stable RL and faithful reasoning.
- RL improves the quality of moves and reduces hallucination rates.
- Metrics such as evaluation performance, hallucination rates, and reasoning quality predict post-RL model performance accurately.

Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

**arXiv:2604.05125v1**

Abstract: Prior authorization (PA) involves interpreting complex and fragmented coverage policies. Current retrieval-augmented systems use static top-$K$ strategies which can be inefficient and gather irrelevant or insufficient information. This research models policy retrieval for PA as a sequential decision-making problem formulated as a Markov Decision Process (MDP). An agent selects policy chunks from a top-$K$ set or stops to make a decision. Training was done using Conservative Q-Learning (CQL), Implicit Q-Learning (IQL), and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) in an offline reinforcement learning setting. Results show CQL achieved 92% decision accuracy through exhaustive retrieval, while IQL matched baseline accuracy with fewer retrieval steps and positive episodic returns. DPO also achieved high accuracy with fewer retrieval steps compared to CQL.

Title:Uncertainty-Guided Latent Diagnostic Trajectory Learning for Sequential Clinical Diagnosis

Abstract:Clinical diagnosis often involves sequential evidence gathering under uncertainty. Current Large Language Model (LLM)-based diagnostic systems typically assume complete patient information, failing to model sequential evidence acquisition effectively. This research introduces a Latent Diagnostic Trajectory Learning (LDTL) framework using a planning LLM and a diagnostic LLM. The diagnostic LLM treats diagnostic actions as latent paths and uses a posterior distribution to prioritize informative trajectories. The planning LLM is trained to follow this distribution, promoting coherent diagnostic trajectories that progressively reduce uncertainty. Experiments on the MIMIC-CDM dataset show improved diagnostic accuracy and reduced test requirements compared to existing methods.

- **タイトル**: Edit, But Verify: An Empirical Audit of Instructed Code-Editing Benchmarks
- **著者**: Amir M. Ebrahimi, Gopi Krishnan Rajbahadur
- **提出日**: 2026年4月6日

**概要**:
- 指示付きコード編集（LLMが自然言語の指示に基づいて既存のコードを修正する）は、実世界のコーディングアシスタントのインタラクションの約19%を占める。
- しかし、この能力を直接評価するベンチマークは非常に少ない。
- 調査によると、150以上のコード関連のベンチマークの中で、人間が作成した指示とテストベースの評価を持つものとして、CanItEditとEDIT-Benchが該当する。
- この研究では、これらのベンチマークのプログラミング言語、編集意図、適用ドメインを対象に調査を行い、テスト数、ステートメントカバレッジ、テストスコープを測定している。
- 両方のベンチマークはPythonに重点を置き、GitHubで最も使用されているTypeScriptは欠如している。
- バックエンドとフロントエンド開発は、実際の編集活動の46%を占めているが、これらは大きく欠落している。
- ドキュメンテーション、テスト、メンテナンス編集（人間のPRの31.4%）は全く表現されていない。
- CanItEditは完全なファイルカバレッジと失敗前の通過後の検証を持っているが、EDIT-Benchは低いカバレッジスコープを持つ。
- EDIT-Benchの15の問題は、40のLLMのいずれも解決できない。これらの問題の多くは、ベンチマークのアーティファクトの品質が悪いことが原因である。
- さらに、EDIT-Benchの29%の問題とCanItEditの6%の問題は、ベンチマーク内の他の問題と共通のコードベースを持っている。

**結論**:
- これらのベンチマークは、デプロイメント決定よりも狭い構造を測定している。
- 作者らは、これらのベンチマークのスコアが実世界の編集能力を正確に反映することを確認するために、6つの経験的な基準を提案している。
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05100v1
**arXiv:2604.05096v1**

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) primarily acquire their knowledge during pretraining, making them tied to a fixed snapshot of the world. This poses challenges when adapting to continuously evolving knowledge. Existing methods like continual fine-tuning, knowledge editing, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) aim to update or supplement model knowledge but are rarely evaluated in real-world settings. The study introduces a new benchmark of real-world dynamic events to evaluate model adaptation under continuous knowledge drift. It reveals that current methods, including vanilla RAG and learning-based approaches, struggle with catastrophic forgetting and temporal inconsistency. A proposed solution, Chronos, uses an Event Evolution Graph to organize retrieved evidence, enabling more temporally consistent understanding in LLMs without additional training.

MegaTrain: Full Precision Training of 100B+ Parameter Large Language Models on a Single GPU

Abstract: Introduces MegaTrain, a memory-centric system designed to efficiently train large language models with over 100 billion parameters at full precision using a single GPU. Unlike traditional GPU-centric approaches, MegaTrain utilizes host memory (CPU memory) for storing parameters and optimizer states, treating GPUs as transient compute engines. Key optimizations include a pipelined double-buffered execution engine and stateless layer templates, which enhance training throughput. On a single H200 GPU with 1.5TB host memory, MegaTrain can reliably train models up to 120B parameters. Compared to DeepSpeed ZeRO-3 with CPU offloading, MegaTrain achieves 1.84 times higher training throughput when training 14B models. Additionally, it supports 7B model training with 512k token context on a single GH200.

**arXiv:2604.05088v1** (eess)

Abstract: We propose ScalarFedLQR, a communication-efficient federated algorithm for model-free learning of a common policy in linear quadratic regulator (LQR) control of heterogeneous agents. The method reduces per-agent uplink communication from O(d) to O(1). As the number of participating agents increases, the approximation error diminishes, allowing for more accurate gradient recovery, larger stepsizes, and faster linear convergence despite high dimensionality. Under standard regularity conditions, all iterates remain stabilizing and the average LQR cost decreases linearly fast. Numerical results show performance comparable to full-gradient federated LQR with substantially reduced communication.

**arXiv:2604.05087v1**

Title: Document Optimization for Black-Box Retrieval via Reinforcement Learning

Abstract: Document expansion is reformulated as a document optimization problem where a language model or a vision-language model is fine-tuned to transform documents into representations that better align with the expected query distribution under a target retriever. The method uses GRPO (Generalized Reward Propagation Optimization) with the retriever's ranking improvements as rewards. It requires only black-box access to retrieval ranks and can be applied to various types of retrievers including single-vector, multi-vector, and lexical retrievers. Evaluations show that optimized documents improve retrieval performance, enabling smaller models to outperform larger ones in some cases. For instance, optimizing documents for the OpenAI text-embedding-3-small model improved its nDCG5 scores on code retrieval and visual document retrieval (VDR), sometimes surpassing the larger OpenAI text-embedding-3-large model. When retriever weights are accessible, document optimization often competes well with fine-tuning, and combining both methods usually yields the best results.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.05087v1

Title: Nidus: Externalized Reasoning for AI-Assisted Engineering

Abstract: We introduce Nidus, a governance runtime that automates the V-model for AI-assisted software delivery. In a self-hosting setup, three large language model families (Claude, Gemini, Codex) developed a 100,000-line system where proof obligations were verified against the current obligation set on every commit. The system managed its own construction. Assurance mechanisms are required externally to maintain engineering invariants such as traced requirements, justified architecture, and evidenced deliveries. Nidus externalizes the engineering methodology into a verifiable artifact checked before each change. Organizational standards are compiled into guidebooks and enforced through decidable evaluations.

Contributions:
1. Recursive self-governance: Constraints limit changes to themselves.
2. Stigmergic coordination: Agents coordinate without central control due to surface friction.
3. Proximal specification reinforcement: The living artifact externalizes the engineering context, making the specification the reward function and shaping behavior during inference.
4. Prevention of compliance fabrication: Compliance evidence cannot be falsified within the modeled mutation path.

The constraint surface accumulates obligations, eliminating classes of unengineered outputs permanently. Each state satisfies all active obligations, and the obligation set grows monotonically throughout the development process.

Title: Hierarchical SVG Tokenization: Learning Compact Visual Programs for Scalable Vector Graphics Modeling

Abstract: Recent advancements in large language models have shifted SVG generation from differential rendering optimization to autoregressive program synthesis. However, current methods still use generic byte-level tokenization from natural language processing, which fails to capture the geometric structure of vector graphics effectively. This leads to fragmented numerical coordinates, disrupting spatial relationships and causing token redundancy. The proposed solution, HiVG, introduces a hierarchical SVG tokenization framework designed specifically for autoregressive vector graphics generation. It decomposes raw SVG strings into structured atomic tokens and compresses executable command-parameter groups into geometry-constrained segment tokens, enhancing sequence efficiency while maintaining syntactic correctness. Additionally, a Hierarchical Mean-Noise (HMN) initialization strategy is introduced to improve token embeddings by injecting numerical ordering signals and semantic priors. Combined with a curriculum training approach that gradually increases program complexity, HiVG facilitates more stable learning of executable SVG programs. Experiments show improved generation fidelity, spatial consistency, and sequence efficiency compared to traditional tokenization methods.

Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG)

**arXiv:2604.05066v1** (cs)

Abstract: Data movement is the primary bottleneck in modern computing systems. For loop-based programs common in high-performance computing (HPC) and AI workloads, including matrix multiplication, tensor contraction, stencil computation, and einsum operations, the cost of moving data through the memory hierarchy often exceeds the cost of arithmetic.

This paper introduces AutoLALA, an open-source tool designed to analyze data locality in affine loop programs. It accepts programs written in a specific domain-specific language (DSL), converts them into polyhedral sets and maps, and generates symbolic formulas for reuse distance and data movement complexity. AutoLALA combines the fully symbolic locality analysis method from Zhu et al. with the data movement distance (DMD) framework from Smith et al., calculating reuse distance without resorting to stack simulation or Denning's recursive working-set formulation.

The DSL syntax and its formal semantics are described, along with the polyhedral lowering pipeline that constructs timestamp spaces and access maps using affine transformations. The tool also employs a series of Barvinok counting operations to derive symbolic reuse-interval and reuse-distance distributions. Implemented in Rust, the system is modular, consisting of three crates with safe bindings to the Barvinok library.

Users can interact with AutoLALA via a command-line interface or an interactive web playground that supports LaTeX rendering of output formulas. The tool is capable of handling arbitrary affine loop nests, encompassing various workloads like tensor contractions, einsum expressions, stencil computations, and general polyhedral programs.

Fine-tuning three foundational models on DynLMC-generated data resulted in consistent improvements in zero-shot forecasting across nine benchmarks. The study highlights the importance of modeling dynamic inter-channel correlations for improving FMTS transferability.

Title:R3PM-Net: Real-time, Robust, Real-world Point Matching Network

Abstract: Accurate Point Cloud Registration (PCR) is an important task in 3D data processing. Traditional methods face challenges like sensitivity to noise, outliers, occlusion, and initialization. R3PM-Net, a lightweight, global-aware, object-level point matching network, addresses these issues by prioritizing generalizability and real-time efficiency. It performs well on imperfect photogrammetric and event-camera scans, achieving high accuracy and unmatched speed. On ModelNet40, it reaches a perfect fitness score of $1$ and inlier RMSE of $0.029$ cm in only $0.007$s, approximately 7 times faster than the state-of-the-art method RegTR. In the Sioux-Cranfield dataset, it maintains a fitness of $1$ and inlier RMSE of $0.030$ cm with low latency. On the challenging Sioux-Scans dataset, it resolves edge cases in under 50 ms.

- **タイトル**: Learning to Unscramble Feynman Loop Integrals with SAILIR
- **著者**: David Shih
- **概要**: 高エネルギー物理学におけるFeynman積分のIBP（Integration-by-parts）簡約は計算上のボトルネックとなっている。伝統的なLaportaアルゴリズムに基づくアプローチでは、複雑さが増すにつれてメモリ消費量が急激に増加する。SAILIR（Self-supervised AI for Loop Integral Reduction）は、トランスフォームベースの分類器を使用して合成データ上で自己教師あり学習を行い、積分をステップバイステップで簡約する新しい機械学習アプローチである。SAILIRは、ビームサーチと並列化された非同期単一エピソード簡約戦略と組み合わせることで、任意の重みを持つ積分を制限されたメモリで簡約できる。ベンチマークでは、Kiraと比較して、最も複雑な積分でもKiraの40％のメモリしか使用せず、同等の簡約時間を達成している。

Phase-Associative Memory (PAM) is a recurrent sequence model using complex-valued representations. Associations accumulate in a matrix state \( S_t \in \mathbb{C}^{d \times d} \) via outer products, and retrieval uses the conjugate inner product \( K_t^* \cdot Q_t / \sqrt{d} \). On WikiText-103, PAM achieves a validation perplexity of 30.0 with approximately 100M parameters, performing within ~10% of a matched transformer under identical conditions despite a 4x arithmetic overhead from complex computation. The study traces the development from vector-state models where holographic binding fails due to capacity degradation, to matrix states resolving these issues. The research suggests that non-classical contextuality in semantic interpretation may favor complex-valued computational formalisms in language modeling.

Title: Early Stopping for Large Reasoning Models via Confidence Dynamics

Abstract: Large reasoning models depend on extensive chain-of-thought generation to solve complex problems, which often leads to significant computational costs and potential performance degradation due to overthinking. The main challenge lies in determining the optimal stopping point for reasoning. This research observes that correct reasoning paths typically reach high-confidence answers quickly, whereas incorrect paths tend to generate lengthy, unproductive reasoning sequences with unreliable confidence levels. Based on these findings, the authors introduce CoDE-Stop (Confidence Dynamics Early Stop), a method that uses the dynamics of intermediate answer confidence to determine the best time to halt reasoning. This approach does not require additional training and can be easily integrated into existing models. Evaluations show that CoDE-Stop offers a better balance between accuracy and computational efficiency, reducing overall token usage by 25-50% compared to full-length reasoning. Additionally, the study provides detailed analyses of confidence dynamics during reasoning, offering insights into how confidence evolves in both successful and unsuccessful reasoning processes.
Subjects: Computation and Language (cs.CL); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Title: Rethinking Model Efficiency: Multi-Agent Inference with Large Models

Abstract: Most vision-language models (VLMs) use a large language model (LLM) as the decoder, generating response tokens sequentially. This sequential generation can limit the end-to-end latency due to the number of output tokens. The study finds that a large model with fewer output tokens can be more efficient than a smaller model with longer sequences. Empirical studies confirm that a large model can achieve similar performance with significantly fewer output tokens compared to a small model. The authors propose a multi-agent inference framework to enhance the efficiency of large models by keeping short responses while transferring key reasoning tokens from smaller models when necessary.

Title:Stratifying Reinforcement Learning with Signal Temporal Logic

Abstract:This paper introduces a stratification-based approach for interpreting Signal Temporal Logic (STL), where each predicate corresponds to a membership test within a stratified space. This method reveals a novel connection between stratification theory and STL, indicating that STL formulas can define stratifications of space-time. The research proposes a theoretical framework for analyzing the embedding space from deep reinforcement learning (DRL) and its relation to the decision space's geometry. Additionally, it suggests a framework for applying existing high-dimensional analysis tools and developing new computational methods. The study demonstrates the application of stratification theory in Minigrid games and uses numerical techniques to evaluate the robustness of STL formulas as rewards in DRL scenarios. Preliminary results suggest promising computational signatures for identifying stratification structures in embedding spaces.

**arXiv:2604.04921v1** (cs.CL; cs.CV)

Abstract Reasoning in large language models leads to significant KV cache memory issues. Current KV cache compression techniques rely on estimating KV importance based on recent attention scores, but this approach fails due to the rotation of queries during RoPE. The proposed TriAttention method addresses this by focusing on the pre-RoPE space where Q and K vectors concentrate around stable centers. This concentration allows queries to prefer keys at specific distances, enabling more efficient key importance estimation through a trigonometric series. TriAttention achieves comparable reasoning accuracy to Full Attention while offering 2.5x higher throughput or 10.7x KV memory reduction. It enables deployment of OpenClaw on a single consumer GPU without running into memory limitations.

Authors: Weian Mao, Xi Lin, Wei Huang, Yuxin Xie, Tianfu Fu, Bohan Zhuang, Song Han, Yukang Chen

Title: Vero: An Open RL Recipe for General Visual Reasoning

Abstract: This paper introduces Vero, a family of fully open vision-language models (VLMs) designed to match or exceed existing open-weight models across various visual reasoning tasks. Key contributions include scaling reinforcement learning (RL) data and rewards across six broad task categories, creating a 600K-sample dataset called Vero-600K from 59 datasets, and designing task-specific rewards to handle diverse answer formats. Vero demonstrates state-of-the-art performance, improving by 3.6-5.3 points on average across 30 challenging benchmarks in VeroEval compared to four base models. It also outperforms Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking on 23 out of 30 benchmarks when trained from the same base model, Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct. Systematic ablation studies indicate that broad data coverage is crucial for effective RL scaling. All data, code, and models are publicly available.

Title: Analyzing Symbolic Properties for DRL Agents in Systems and Networking

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has demonstrated significant performance in complex control problems within systems and networking, such as adaptive video streaming, wireless resource management, and congestion control. To ensure safe deployment, it is crucial to understand agent behavior across various system states. Current verification methods focus on point properties, offering limited coverage and requiring substantial manual effort. This paper introduces symbolic properties, specifying expected behaviors over ranges of input states, and presents a generic formulation with monotonicity and robustness as examples. The authors demonstrate how these properties can be analyzed using existing deep neural network (DNN) verification engines. They use their framework, diffRL, to conduct extensive empirical studies across three DRL-based control systems, highlighting broader coverage and uncovering non-obvious operational counterexamples.

Title: HI-MoE: Hierarchical Instance-Conditioned Mixture-of-Experts for Object Detection

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures enable conditional computation by activating only a subset of model parameters for each input. The proposed Hierarchical Instance-Conditioned Mixture-of-Experts (HI-MoE) operates in two stages: a lightweight scene router selects a scene-consistent expert subset, followed by an instance router assigning each object query to a small number of experts within that subset. HI-MoE shows improvements over a dense DINO baseline and simpler routing variants, particularly for small objects, based on experiments conducted on COCO and LVIS datasets.

Title: Are Latent Reasoning Models Easily Interpretable?

Abstract: Latent reasoning models (LRMs) have gained attention due to their low inference costs and ability to explore multiple reasoning paths simultaneously. However, their interpretability is compromised as they do not reason in natural language. The study examines two advanced LRMs and finds that latent reasoning tokens are often unnecessary for accurate predictions. When necessary, these tokens can decode gold reasoning traces up to 65-93% of the time for correct predictions. The researchers also present a method to decode verified natural language reasoning traces from latent tokens, indicating that LRMs generally implement understandable reasoning processes.

Abstract:Coworking AI agents operating within local file systems are becoming a key paradigm in human-AI interaction but face limitations due to data constraints caused by privacy barriers and difficulties in collecting multimodal real-world traces. To tackle this issue, the authors propose FileGram, a framework that personalizes AI agents based on file-system behavioral traces. The framework includes:
1. FileGramEngine: A scalable data engine that simulates realistic workflows and generates large-scale multimodal action sequences.
2. FileGramBench: A benchmark for evaluating memory systems using file-system behavioral traces.
3. FileGramOS: A memory architecture that builds user profiles from atomic actions and content changes, encoding these into procedural, semantic, and episodic channels.

Experiments demonstrate that FileGramBench poses significant challenges for current memory systems, while FileGramEngine and FileGramOS prove to be effective solutions. The authors aim to support future research by making the framework available.

Title: QED-Nano: Teaching a Tiny Model to Prove Hard Theorems

Abstract: Proprietary AI systems have shown impressive capabilities in complex proof-based problems, achieving gold-level performance at the 2025 IMO. However, details of their training processes are not disclosed due to reliance on large internal models. This paper explores whether smaller, open-source models can achieve competitive performance on Olympiad-level math problems. The researchers developed QED-Nano, a 4B model trained specifically for Olympiad-level proofs through three stages: supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning with rubric-based rewards, and expanding RL with a reasoning cache. QED-Nano outperforms larger open models like Nomos-1 and GPT-OSS-120B and nearly matches proprietary models such as Gemini 3 Pro, all while reducing inference costs significantly. The full QED-Nano pipeline, including models, datasets, and code, is released to support further research in open mathematical reasoning.

Abstract: Although Federated Learning (FL) offers privacy and distributed collaboration benefits, its performance in real-world applications is often compromised by the stochastic variability among clients and unpredictable system dynamics. **Title:** DIRECT: Video Mashup Creation via Hierarchical Multi-Agent Planning and Intent-Guided Editing

**Abstract:** Video mashup creation involves reassembling existing footage to create engaging audio-visual experiences. Current automated editing frameworks often fail to achieve seamless integration across semantic, visual, and auditory dimensions. The DIRECT framework addresses this issue by formulating video mashup creation as a Multimodal Coherency Satisfaction Problem (MMCSP). It uses a hierarchical multi-agent system with three levels: the Screenwriter for global structural anchoring, the Director for adaptive editing intent, and the Editor for fine-tuned shot sequence editing. The team also introduced Mashup-Bench, a benchmark with metrics for visual continuity and auditory alignment. Experiments show that DIRECT outperforms existing methods in both quantitative metrics and subjective evaluations.

**arXiv:2604.04874v1** (cs.CV)

Abstract: We present Free-Range Gaussians, a method that reconstructs non-pixel, non-voxel-aligned 3D Gaussians from as few as four images using flow matching over Gaussian parameters. The method allows supervision with non-grid-aligned 3D data and synthesizes plausible content in unobserved regions. It addresses limitations of previous methods producing redundant grid-aligned Gaussians, which often suffer from holes or blurry areas in unobserved regions. The approach introduces a hierarchical patching scheme to manage the number of required Gaussians efficiently and proposes a timestep-weighted rendering loss, photometric gradient guidance, and classifier-free guidance to enhance reconstruction quality. Experimental results on Objaverse and Google Scanned Objects demonstrate significant improvements over pixel and voxel-aligned methods, requiring fewer Gaussians and showing substantial gains when input views leave parts of the object unobserved.

**arXiv:2604.04872v1** (cs.CL; cs.LG)

Abstract: The paper introduces SandMLE, a multi-agent framework designed to generate synthetic environments for training machine learning engineering agents. This framework addresses the high cost of verifying agent behavior in machine learning engineering (MLE) tasks compared to software engineering (SWE) tasks. While SWE tasks can be verified using fast-executing unit tests, MLE verification requires running full ML pipelines on large datasets, making trajectory-wise on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) impractical due to its high computational expense.

Key points:
- SandMLE generates diverse, verifiable synthetic MLE environments from a small number of seed tasks.
- It preserves the structural and technical complexity of real-world problems but constrains datasets to micro-scale (50-200 training samples per task).
- Experiments show that SandMLE reduces execution time by over 13 times, enabling large-scale, on-policy trajectory-wise RL for the first time in the MLE domain.
- On MLE-bench-lite, SandMLE shows significant gains over supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baselines across Qwen3-8B, 14B, and 30B-A3B models, with relative medal rate improvements ranging from 20.3% to 66.9%.
- The trained policy generalizes well, achieving up to 32.4% better HumanRank score on MLE-Dojo.

Subjects: Computation and Language (cs.CL); Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Comments: 28 pages, 9 tables, 8 figures

Title: Less Detail, Better Answers: Degradation-Driven Prompting for VQA

Abstract: Recent advancements in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have improved Visual Question Answering (VQA). However, high-resolution details can lead to hallucinations or reasoning errors. The paper proposes Degradation-Driven Prompting (DDP), a framework that enhances VQA performance by reducing image fidelity to focus on essential structural information. DDP evaluates its effectiveness through two tasks: Physical attributes and Perceptual phenomena. For Physical attributes, it uses techniques like 80p downsampling, white background masks, and In-Context Learning (ICL). Experimental results show that DDP improves reasoning accuracy by degrading visual inputs and providing targeted structural prompts.

Title:Comparative Characterization of KV Cache Management Strategies for LLM Inference

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on reasoning benchmarks but their capability to solve real-world problems through end-to-end workflows is still uncertain. This study introduces a problem-oriented, stage-wise evaluation framework to assess LLM performance against verified criteria at each modeling stage. The framework's reliability was confirmed by comparing automated scores with independent human expert judgments on problems from the China Postgraduate Mathematical Contest in Modeling, showing significantly better alignment compared to existing evaluation methods. The research reveals a comprehension-execution gap in advanced LLMs: while they excel in initial stages like problem identification and formulation, they consistently underperform in execution stages such as model solving, coding, and result analysis. This gap persists even with larger model scales. Title: CLEAR: Unlocking Generative Potential for Degraded Image Understanding in Unified Multimodal Models

Abstract: This paper addresses the issue of image degradation caused by factors such as blur, noise, compression, and poor lighting. It introduces CLEAR, a framework designed to enhance the generative potential of unified multimodal models for understanding degraded images. The framework consists of three progressive steps:
1. Supervised fine-tuning on a degradation-aware dataset to establish a generate-then-answer reasoning pattern.
2. A Latent Representation Bridge that optimizes the connection between generation and reasoning.
3. Interleaved GRPO, a reinforcement learning method that optimizes text reasoning and visual generation based on answer correctness rewards.

Experiments conducted on MMD-Bench, which covers three levels of degradation severity across six standard multimodal benchmarks, demonstrate that CLEAR significantly improves robustness on degraded inputs without compromising performance on clean images. The study also finds that removing pixel-level reconstruction supervision results in higher perceptual quality in intermediate visual states.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04780v1
