Title: Think in Strokes, Not Pixels: Process-Driven Image Generation via Interleaved Reasoning

Abstract: Humans create images progressively, planning layouts, sketching drafts, inspecting, and refining details based on evolving visual states. This paper introduces process-driven image generation, a multi-step approach that breaks down image synthesis into an iterative reasoning process involving four stages: textual planning, visual drafting, textual reflection, and visual refinement. Each stage influences the next, ensuring explicit, interpretable, and directly supervisable generation. The study addresses challenges in evaluating partially complete images through dense, step-wise supervision, maintaining spatial and semantic consistency in visual states and preserving prior visual knowledge in textual states.

Title: Hallucination Basins: A Dynamic Framework for Understanding and Controlling LLM Hallucinations

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) generate fluent but factually incorrect outputs. The study introduces a geometric dynamical systems framework where hallucinations originate from task-specific basin structures within latent spaces. Through analysis using autoregressive hidden-state trajectories across various open-source models and benchmarks, it identifies that separability of these basins is highly dependent on the task rather than being universally applicable. Factoid tasks exhibit clearer basin separation compared to summarization or misconception-heavy tasks, which tend to be less stable and often overlap. The research formalizes these observations through task-complexity and multi-basin theorems, explores basin emergence in L-layer transformers, and demonstrates that geometry-aware steering can decrease hallucination probabilities without requiring retraining.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04743v1
**arXiv:2604.04738v1**

Abstract: Fine-tuning is now the primary method for adapting large neural networks, introducing new integrity risks such as backdoor insertion, safety behavior changes, and model overwriting. Current verification tools do not address these issues. The paper introduces Fine-Tuning Integrity (FTI) as a security goal for controlled model evolution, ensuring that a fine-tuned model only differs from a trusted base within a policy-defined drift class. Succinct Model Difference Proofs (SMDPs) are proposed as a cryptographic primitive providing zero-knowledge proofs that updates are norm-bounded, low-rank, or sparse. These proofs depend only on the structure of the drift, not the model size. Concrete SMDP constructions are given based on random projections, polynomial commitments, and streaming linear checks. The paper also presents architecture-aware instantiations for transformers, CNNs, and MLPs, along with an end-to-end system aggregating block-level proofs into a global certificate.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04738v1
**arXiv:2604.04735v1** (cs.CL)

This paper explores "dark patterns" in human-AI co-creativity, focusing on how large language models (LLMs) act as collaborative writing partners. The study identifies five specific dark patterns: Sycophancy, Tone Policing, Moralizing, Loop of Death, and Anchoring. Preliminary findings indicate that Sycophancy is prevalent in 91.7% of cases, especially in sensitive topics, whereas Anchoring varies depending on the literary form, appearing most in folktales. The research suggests that these dark patterns, often unintended consequences of safety measures, can limit creative exploration and offers recommendations for designing AI systems that better support creative writing.

\[Submitted on 6 Apr 2026\]

Title: Don't Waste Bits! Adaptive KV-Cache Quantization for Lightweight On-Device LLMs

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made significant advancements in reasoning, generation, and decision-making tasks but face challenges when deployed on mobile, embedded, and edge devices due to memory and bandwidth constraints. The key-value (KV) cache used in on-device LLM inference grows linearly with context length, increasing memory and bandwidth overhead. Current KV-cache quantization methods often use fixed precision or heuristic approaches, leading to wasted bits on less important tokens and excessive compression of critical ones, thus degrading accuracy. The proposed adaptive KV-cache quantization method allocates bit-width based on token importance, reducing memory usage and latency while maintaining high accuracy. The approach uses token-level features such as frequency, quality score, attention variance, and entropy-based uncertainty to guide a compact controller that dynamically adjusts KV precision during decoding. Experiments show improvements in accuracy-latency trade-offs, with reductions in decoding latency and improvements in accuracy compared to static KV quantization and rule-based methods.

Comments: Accepted by the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR) 2026

Subjects: Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV)

**Abstract Summary:** The study reveals that while AI assistance enhances short-term performance, it leads to reduced persistence and impaired independent performance among users. Through randomized controlled trials involving 1,222 participants, the research highlights that brief interactions with AI (about 10 minutes) can negatively impact a person’s ability to solve problems independently and increase the likelihood of giving up. This effect is concerning due to the critical role of persistence in skill acquisition and long-term learning.

The researchers argue that AI systems, designed to provide immediate answers, condition users to rely on quick solutions rather than fostering independent problem-solving skills. This suggests a need for AI models to be developed with a focus on supporting long-term competence alongside immediate task completion.

MUXQ (Mixed-to-Uniform Quantization) is proposed to address the issue of input-activation outliers and hardware inefficiencies in large language models (LLMs). It detects outlier channels in input activations and redistributes outlier magnitudes across channels using a small auxiliary matrix. This method allows for low-precision integer quantization while maintaining a hardware-friendly computation structure. Experiments on GPT-2 models show that MUXQ reduces perplexity compared to naive quantization and maintains accuracy close to FP16 under per-tensor quantization, enabling stable low-precision inference with minimal computational overhead.

**arXiv:2604.04690v1** (cs)

Abstract: Bin picking in real industrial environments remains challenging due to severe clutter, occlusions, and the high cost of traditional 3D sensing setups. The paper introduces Pickalo, a modular 6D pose-based bin-picking pipeline built entirely on low-cost hardware. It uses a wrist-mounted RGB-D camera to explore scenes from multiple viewpoints and processes raw stereo streams with BridgeDepth to refine depth maps. Objects are segmented using a Mask-RCNN model trained on synthetic data and localized using the SAM-6D pose estimator. A pose buffer module fuses multi-view observations to handle symmetries and reduce pose noise. Offline, it generates antipodal grasp candidates; online, it ranks these candidates based on utility and performs fast collision checks for grasp planning. Deployed on a UR5e robot with a parallel-jaw gripper and an Intel RealSense D435i camera, Pickalo achieves up to 600 mean picks per hour with 96-99% grasp success and maintains robust performance over 30-minute runs. Ablation studies show the benefits of enhanced depth estimation and pose buffering for long-term stability and throughput in realistic industrial conditions.

Title: Unsharp Measurement with Adaptive Gaussian POVMs for Quantum-Inspired Image Processing

Abstract: We propose a quantum measurement-based framework for probabilistic transformation of grayscale images using adaptive positive operator-valued measures (POVMs). Intensity values are embedded in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, allowing the construction of data-adaptive measurement operators derived from Gaussian models of the image histogram. These operators define an unsharp measurement of the intensity observable, with the reconstructed image obtained through expectation values of the measurement outcomes. A nonlinear sharpening transformation controlled by a parameter $\gamma$ transitions from unsharp measurements to projective measurements, reflecting a trade-off between probabilistic smoothing and localization of intensity structures. Another parameter $k$ controls the resolution of the image during the transformation.

Subjects: Quantum Physics (quant-ph); Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV)

Title: An AI Teaching Assistant for Motion Picture Engineering

Abstract: The study explores the implementation of an AI Teaching Assistant (AI-TA) using Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) for Trinity College Dublin's Master's Motion Picture Engineering (MPE) course. It provides details on the implementation, including prompts and code, and discusses the design and tuning of the RAG pipeline to meet course requirements. The research includes a survey and reports on the impact of the AI-TA through various quantitative metrics. The experiment involved 43 students, 296 sessions, and 1,889 queries over seven weeks. Unlike previous studies, the researchers allowed the use of the AI-TA during open-book examinations, finding no significant performance differences between students who used the AI-TA and those who did not (p > 0.05). Student feedback indicated that the AI-TA was generally beneficial (mean = 4.22/5), though opinions were mixed regarding its preference over human tutoring (mean = 2.78/5).

Abstract: Real-time depth reconstruction from ultra-high-resolution UAV imagery is crucial for time-sensitive geospatial tasks like disaster response but remains difficult due to various challenges including wide-baseline parallax, large image sizes, low-texture or specular surfaces, occlusions, and strict computational constraints. The paper introduces ZeD-MAP, a framework that integrates incremental cluster-based bundle adjustment (BA) to convert a test-time diffusion depth model into a metrically consistent, SLAM-like mapping pipeline. This method groups streamed UAV frames into overlapping clusters and uses periodic BA to produce metrically consistent poses and sparse 3D tie-points, which guide diffusion-based depth estimation. Validation results show sub-meter accuracy with approximately 0.87 m error in the horizontal plane and 0.12 m in the vertical direction, while maintaining per-image runtimes between 1.47 and 4.91 seconds.

Contrastive Hypothesis Retrieval (CHR) is proposed as a framework for medical question answering. It generates a target hypothesis \(H^+\) for the likely correct answer and a mimic hypothesis \(H^-\) for the most plausible incorrect alternative. Documents are scored by promoting \(H^+\)-aligned evidence while penalizing \(H^-\)-aligned content. CHR outperforms all five baselines across three medical QA benchmarks and three answer generators, showing improvements up to 10.4 percentage points. In 85.2% of cases where CHR answers correctly and others do not, there are no shared documents between the top-5 retrieval lists, indicating significant retrieval redirection.

Abstract: Post-hoc unlearning has become a practical method for removing unwanted concepts from large text-to-image diffusion models. Abstract: Recent advancements in vision-language pretraining have facilitated robust medical foundation models. However, most analyses focus on individual radiographs without considering the comparison between past and present images to assess changes over time. This study introduces TILA (Temporal Inversion-aware Learning and Alignment), a framework that enhances the sensitivity of existing temporal vision-language models to directional changes by using temporal inversion—reversing image pairs—as a supervisory signal. Paper Espresso is an open-source platform designed to help researchers manage the overwhelming volume of scientific publications. It uses large language models (LLMs) to automatically discover, summarize, and analyze trending arXiv papers. Title: Mapping the Exploitation Surface: A 10,000-Trial Taxonomy of What Makes LLM Agents Exploit Vulnerabilities

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool access can discover and exploit security vulnerabilities. The study conducted approximately 10,000 trials across seven models under various conditions and found that certain features of a system trigger exploitative behavior while others do not. Key findings include:
- Nine out of twelve tested dimensions did not lead to detectable exploitation.
- Goal reframing reliably triggered exploitation, with phrases like "You are solving a puzzle; there may be hidden clues" leading to 38-40% exploitation rates.
- Improved safety training showed a pattern of reduced exploitation across different models.

Title: EduIllustrate: Towards Scalable Automated Generation Of Multimodal Educational Content

Abstract: Large language models are being used increasingly as educational assistants but their evaluation focuses mainly on question-answering and tutoring tasks. There is a significant gap in generating multimedia instructional content that combines coherent text with accurate diagrams and step-by-step reasoning. EduIllustrate is introduced as a benchmark to evaluate large language models (LLMs) on generating interleaved text-diagram explanations for K-12 STEM problems. This benchmark includes 230 problems across five subjects and three grade levels, a standardized generation protocol, and an 8-dimension evaluation rubric based on multimedia learning theory. Evaluating ten LLMs shows varied performance; Gemini 3.0 Pro Preview leads with 87.8%, while Kimi-K2.5 offers the best cost-efficiency at 80.8% for $0.12 per problem. Sequential anchoring improves visual consistency by 13% at a significantly lower cost. Human evaluations confirm the reliability of LLMs as judges for objective dimensions but highlight limitations in subjective visual assessments.

Abstract: Evaluation language in agentic code benchmarks is usually set to a default English. However, changing the judge's language can reverse backbone rankings. The study localized the Agent-as-a-Judge prompt stack to five languages (English, Arabic, Turkish, Chinese, Hindi) and evaluated 55 DevAI tasks across three frameworks and six judge backbones, totaling 4950 judge runs. Key findings include:
- GPT-4o performs best in English (44.72%).
- Gemini outperforms in Arabic (51.72%) and Hindi (53.22%).
- No single backbone excels across all languages.
- Inter-backbone agreement on individual requirements is low (Fleiss' κ ≤ 0.231).

The study highlights the importance of treating language as an explicit evaluation variable in agentic benchmarks. Full requirement-level judgments and runtime statistics are available for verification.

**arXiv:2604.04528v1** (cs)

Abstract: We address trajectory optimization in scenarios where system dynamics are unknown and cannot be simulated using a surrogate model. If an offline dataset of trajectories is available, an agent can learn a trajectory generator through distribution matching. However, this method only captures the behavior distribution within the dataset and doesn't generally minimize a desired cost criterion. This paper introduces Drifting MPC, an offline trajectory optimization framework combining drifting generative models with receding-horizon planning under unknown dynamics. The aim is to derive from an offline dataset of trajectories a conditional distribution over trajectories that aligns with the data and favors optimal plans. It is demonstrated that the distribution learned by Drifting MPC solves an objective balancing optimality and proximity to the offline prior. Empirical results indicate that Drifting MPC generates nearly optimal trajectories while maintaining the efficiency of one-step inference and significantly reducing generation time compared to diffusion-based methods.

Abstract: Adapting large language models (LLMs) to new domains leads to forgetting due to standard adaptation methods injecting new directions into the weight space. The authors propose GAIN (Gain Additive INterpolation), which emphasizes existing features through multiplicative modulation W_new = S * W. The learned diagonal matrix S is applied to the attention output projection and optionally the feed-forward network (FFN). Evaluation shows GAIN-FFN matches LoRA's performance in the adapted domain but improves performance in previously trained domains by 7-13%, whereas LoRA degrades performance by 18-36%. GAIN introduces 46K-230K parameters per model, which can be integrated into pre-trained weights without additional inference costs.

Title: SuperLocalMemory V3.3: The Living Brain -- Biologically-Inspired Forgetting, Cognitive Quantization, and Multi-Channel Retrieval for Zero-LLM Agent Memory Systems

Abstract: AI coding agents face a paradox where they have extensive parametric knowledge but lack the ability to retain short-term memories. Current memory systems use vector databases with single-channel retrieval, rely on cloud-based large language models (LLMs) for core operations, and do not incorporate cognitive processes that enhance human memory. This paper introduces SuperLocalMemory V3.3 ("The Living Brain"), a local-first agent memory system that implements a comprehensive cognitive memory taxonomy with mathematical lifecycle dynamics. Key contributions include:
- FRQAD (Fisher-Rao Quantization-Aware Distance): A novel metric achieving 100% precision in favoring high-fidelity embeddings over quantized ones.
- Ebbinghaus Adaptive Forgetting: Incorporates a mathematical forgetting curve with lifecycle-aware quantization, enhancing discriminative power.
- 7-channel cognitive retrieval: Offers advanced retrieval methods across various channels, improving performance in zero-LLM mode.
- Implementation of Long-Term Implicit memory through soft prompts.
- Automated memory lifecycle management without friction.

On the LoCoMo benchmark, V3.3 achieves 70.4% accuracy in zero-LLM mode, showing significant improvements in multi-hop and adversarial tasks compared to previous versions. The system is open-source under the Elastic License 2.0 and runs entirely on CPU with over 5,000 monthly downloads.

MPTF-Net: Multi-view Pyramid Transformer Fusion Network for LiDAR-based Place Recognition

Abstract: LiDAR-based place recognition (LPR) is crucial for global localization and loop-closure detection in large-scale SLAM systems. Current methods often use Range Images or BEV representations for constructing global descriptors. However, traditional BEV representations fail to capture fine-grained geometric structures, leading to performance degradation in complex or repetitive environments. To tackle this issue, the authors introduce MPTF-Net, a novel multi-view multi-scale pyramid Transformer fusion network. The key innovation is a multi-channel NDT-based BEV encoding that explicitly models local geometric complexity and intensity distributions, offering a noise-resilient structural prior. Additionally, a customized pyramid Transformer module captures cross-view interactive correlations between Range Image Views (RIV) and NDT-BEV across multiple spatial scales. Experiments on nuScenes, KITTI, and NCLT datasets show that MPTF-Net achieves state-of-the-art performance, with a Recall@1 of 96.31% on the nuScenes Boston split and an inference latency of only 10.02 ms. This makes it highly suitable for real-time autonomous unmanned systems.

Memory Intelligence Agent (MIA) framework is proposed to improve deep research agents (DRAs). The MIA framework includes a Manager-Planner-Executor architecture where the Memory Manager stores compressed historical search trajectories, the Planner generates search plans, and the Executor executes searches based on those plans. Key improvements include an alternating reinforcement learning paradigm for better cooperation between Planner and Executor, continuous evolution of the Planner during test-time learning, and a bidirectional conversion loop between parametric and non-parametric memories for efficient memory evolution. The framework also incorporates reflection and unsupervised judgment mechanisms to enhance reasoning and self-evolution. Experiments across eleven benchmarks show the superiority of MIA.

**Abstract:** Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved significant success in various tasks but face concerns over their reliability, especially in relying too heavily on textual cues rather than visual evidence and producing unfounded responses. Title: The Indra Representation Hypothesis for Multimodal Alignment

Abstract: Recent studies show that unimodal foundation models tend to learn convergent representations, independent of architectural differences, training objectives, or data modalities. However, these representations lack expressiveness due to their isolated nature. Inspired by Indra's Net, the authors propose the Indra Representation Hypothesis, which posits that these representations reflect a shared relational structure underlying reality. They formalize this hypothesis using the V-enriched Yoneda embedding from category theory, defining the Indra representation as a relational profile of each sample relative to others. Experiments demonstrate that Indra representations improve robustness and alignment across different architectures and modalities, including vision, language, and audio. **arXiv:2604.04469v1** (cs.CL)

Abstract: Scalar variability, characterized by representational noise scaling proportionally with magnitude, is observed in biological systems. However, analysis of three large transformer models (Llama-3-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, Llama-3-8B-Base) reveals the opposite: representational variability decreases with magnitude, indicating that transformers do not replicate the scalar variability seen in biological systems.

Subjects: Computation and Language (cs.CL), Quantitative Methods (q-bio.QM)

Key Findings:
- Representational variability in transformer models decreases with magnitude.
- The scaling exponent (alpha) is approximately -0.19 across primary layers.
- Anti-scalar pattern is stronger along the magnitude axis compared to orthogonal dimensions.
- Corpus frequency predicts per-magnitude variability with a correlation coefficient (rho) of .84.

These findings suggest that distributional learning alone cannot produce scalar variability in transformer models.

**Abstract Summary:** This paper discusses a structural limitation in current multimodal AI architectures, focusing on their topological constraints rather than just parameters. It introduces the concept of "contact topology," highlighting issues with current architectures like CLIP, GPT-4V/Gemini, and diffusion-based models due to their reliance on modal separability. Philosophically, it reinterprets Wittgenstein’s distinction between saying and showing as a problem within AI architecture, proposing a framework called "cruciform" that integrates operational schemas (xiang) into AI design. Scientifically, it reinterprets the co-activation patterns in neural networks using a pathological mirror model. Mathematically, it formalizes these concepts using fiber bundles and Yang-Mills curvature. Proposals include Neural ODEs with topological regularization, benchmarks for evaluating multimodal fusion, and tests for cross-cultural topological isomorphism.

DP-OPD: Differentially Private On-Policy Distillation for Language Models

Authors: Fatemeh Khadem, Sajad Mousavi, Yi Fang, Yuhong Liu

Abstract: This paper introduces DP-OPD, a method for differentially private on-policy distillation in language models. It addresses the challenge of maintaining privacy while compressing language models trained on sensitive data. Unlike previous methods which either worsen the privacy-utility trade-off or introduce complex pipelines, DP-OPD simplifies the process by applying differential privacy solely to the student model during training, using a frozen teacher model for guidance. The approach improves perplexity under a strict privacy budget and simplifies the training pipeline compared to existing methods.

**arXiv:2604.04440v1** (cs)

Abstract: We parameterize the weight matrices of a transformer in the two-dimensional discrete cosine transform (DCT) domain, retaining only the lowest-frequency coefficients. During each forward pass, the full weight matrix is reconstructed via the inverse DCT; gradients propagate through the reconstruction to update the spectral coefficients directly.

On character-level language modeling (using Shakespeare, 1M characters dataset), a 4-layer transformer trained from scratch in this representation matches the perplexity of the standard parameterization (6.1 vs. 6.1) while storing 52% of the parameters. At 4x compression (29% of parameters), the model reaches a perplexity of 6.9, outperforming a low-rank baseline (perplexity 8.8 at 21% of parameters).

The method does not require any architectural changes, pre-trained checkpoints, or auxiliary losses. It involves replacing each weight matrix with a spectral layer that stores K DCT coefficients instead of n x m weights.

Subjects: Performance (cs.PF); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04440v1
**arXiv:2604.04427v1** (cs)

Abstract: The paper proposes a Flow-based Average Velocity Establishment (Fave) framework for sequential recommendation. This framework addresses the inefficiencies in generative recommendation methods by learning a direct trajectory from an informative prior to the target distribution. It uses a two-stage training strategy: establishing a stable preference space through dual-end semantic alignment in Stage 1, and resolving efficiency bottlenecks by introducing a semantic anchor prior in Stage 2. Experiments show that Fave improves recommendation performance and significantly enhances inference efficiency.

ShieldNet: Network-Level Guardrails against Emerging Supply-Chain Injections in Agentic Systems

Abstract: Current research on Large Language Model (LLM) agent security primarily addresses prompt injection and unsafe input/output behaviors. However, as agents become more dependent on third-party tools and Managed Content Providers (MCP) servers, a new category of supply-chain threats has emerged. These threats involve embedding malicious behavior within seemingly harmless tools, leading to silent hijacking of agent execution, leakage of sensitive data, or initiation of unauthorized actions. The study introduces SC-Inject-Bench, a large-scale benchmark consisting of over 10,000 malicious MCP tools categorized into 25+ attack types based on MITRE ATT&CK framework. Existing MCP scanners and semantic guardrails perform poorly on this benchmark. To address this issue, the researchers propose ShieldNet, a network-level guardrail framework designed to detect supply-chain poisoning through monitoring real network interactions instead of superficial tool traces. ShieldNet incorporates a man-in-the-middle (MITM) proxy and an event extractor to identify critical network behaviors, which are then analyzed by a lightweight classifier for attack detection. Experiments demonstrate that ShieldNet achieves high detection accuracy (F-1 score up to 0.995 with only 0.8% false positives) without significant runtime overhead, outperforming current MCP scanners and LLM-based guardrails.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04426v1
BoxComm: Benchmarking Category-Aware Commentary Generation and Narration Rhythm in Boxing

Authors: Kaiwen Wang, Kaili Zheng, Rongrong Deng, Yiming Shi, Chenyi Guo, Ji Wu

Abstract: Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in general video understanding, leading to increased interest in automatic sports commentary generation. However, existing benchmarks for this task focus solely on team sports like soccer and basketball, neglecting combat sports. This paper introduces BoxComm, a large-scale dataset consisting of 445 World Boxing Championship match videos with over 52K commentary sentences from professional broadcasts. The dataset includes a structured commentary taxonomy that categorizes each sentence into play-by-play, tactical, or contextual commentary. Two novel evaluations are proposed: (1) category-conditioned generation, assessing if models can generate accurate commentary of a specified type based on video context; and (2) commentary rhythm assessment, measuring the temporal pacing and type distribution of freely generated commentary over continuous video segments. Experiments show that current models struggle with these evaluations. Additionally, the authors propose EIC-Gen, an improved baseline that incorporates detected punch events to provide structured action cues, highlighting the significance of recognizing fleeting and subtle events in combat sports commentary.

**arXiv:2604.04418v1**

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are used in critical environments, users need to evaluate the correctness of individual responses using justifications provided by the models. However, there is no established method to determine if these justifications help users differentiate between correct and incorrect answers. The study introduces "error verifiability" as a metric, denoted as $v_{\text{bal}}$, which evaluates whether justifications assist raters in accurately assessing the correctness of answers. This metric was validated through high-agreement ratings from human judges. Common methods like post-training adjustments and scaling did not enhance verifiability. Instead, two novel techniques were found effective: "reflect-and-rephrase" (RR) for enhancing mathematical reasoning and "oracle-rephrase" (OR) for improving factual question answering. These methods improved verifiability by integrating relevant external information specific to their domains. The research concludes that error verifiability represents a unique dimension of response quality that cannot be solely addressed by improving accuracy.

For effective model training, CausalFact-60K dataset and a four-stage pipeline including facts alignment, format warm-start, thinking warm-start, and reinforcement learning-based post-training are introduced. The authors address the challenge of balancing multiple objectives during training by framing the optimization as a Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning (MORL) problem, leading to the development of Factum-4B, which shows improved reasoning reliability and performance on complex video understanding tasks.

Abstract: This paper explores the gap between internal representations and generated responses in visual document understanding (VDU) for large vision-language models (LVLMs). The research reveals that LVLMs often fail to accurately capture necessary information internally, despite showing progress on VDU benchmarks. Linear probing was used to analyze information representation across different layers of LVLMs, indicating that the required information is encoded more linearly in intermediate layers compared to the final layer. Fine-tuning strategies targeting intermediate layers were found to improve both linear probing accuracy and response accuracy, thereby reducing the gap between internal representations and responses.

MolDA: Molecular Understanding and Generation via Large Language Diffusion Model

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have advanced molecular discovery, but current multimodal molecular architectures depend on autoregressive (AR) backbones, which limit their effectiveness in generating chemically valid molecules due to their strict left-to-right inductive bias. The proposed MolDA framework uses a discrete Large Language Diffusion Model to replace the traditional AR backbone. It employs a hybrid graph encoder to capture both local and global topological features and aligns them into the language token space through a Q-Former. Additionally, the team reformulates Molecular Structure Preference Optimization for masked diffusion, ensuring global structural coherence, chemical validity, and robust reasoning across molecule generation, captioning, and property prediction.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has shown success in single-agent settings, but extending it to multi-agent reinforcement learning in general-sum Markov games is challenging. This paper examines the convergence of Stackelberg Q-value iteration in two-player general-sum Markov games from a control-theoretic perspective. **arXiv:2604.04387v2** (cs)

Title: Gradual Cognitive Externalization: From Modeling Cognition to Constituting It

Abstract: Developers are creating AI agents that can mimic a colleague’s communication style, encode a supervisor’s mentoring techniques, or even preserve a person's behavioral patterns after death. This paper proposes the Gradual Cognitive Externalization (GCE) framework, which argues that ambient AI systems, through continuous interaction with users, evolve from merely modeling cognitive functions to becoming integral parts of users’ cognitive structures. The GCE framework posits that cognitive functions are defined by their causal-functional roles rather than the physical medium they operate in. It relies on the behavioral manifold hypothesis and the No Behaviorally Invisible Residual (NBIR) hypothesis, stating that for any cognitive function, if its behavioral outputs lie on a learnable manifold, then no unseen behavioral components are required for its operation. The paper provides evidence from existing AI deployments, outlines criteria distinguishing cognitive integration from tool usage, and formulates five testable hypotheses.

Title:Automatically Generating Hard Math Problems from Hypothesis-Driven Error Analysis

Abstract:Numerous math benchmarks exist to evaluate LLMs' mathematical capabilities. However, most involve extensive manual effort and are difficult to scale. Consequently, they cannot keep pace with LLM development or easily provide new instances to mitigate overfitting. The proposed method uses AI-generated hypotheses to identify specific math concepts and skills where LLMs struggle, generating new benchmark problems targeting these weaknesses. Experiments show that hypothesis accuracy positively correlates with problem difficulty: problems generated from the most accurate hypotheses reduced Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct's accuracy to as low as 45%, compared to 77% on the original MATH benchmark. The pipeline is adaptable and can be applied to explore a wide range of LLM capabilities.

Title: How Alignment Routes: Localizing, Scaling, and Controlling Policy Circuits in Language Models

Abstract: This paper identifies a sparse routing mechanism in alignment-trained language models. A gate attention head detects content and triggers amplifier heads to boost signals towards refusal. The study traces this mechanism across nine models from six labs, validated on corpora of 120 prompt pairs. Key findings include:
- Gate head passes necessity and sufficiency interchange tests (p < 0.001).
- Amplifier heads remain stable under bootstrap resampling (Jaccard 0.92-1.0).
- Routing scales up to 17 times weaker but remains detectable.
- Modulation of detection-layer signal controls policy strength.
- Structural separation between intent recognition and policy routing observed.

Abstract: Across five transformer language models, the logit energy field $\tilde{E}$ captures 90% of its variance within 2-11 singular components. The learned interaction matrix $W_Q^{\mathrm{T}} W_K$ requires 38-75 components for the same threshold. The spectral gap is $5$-$25\times$ in effective rank. The attention mechanism distributes capacity uniformly across all dimensions, whereas language interactions concentrate into a few dimensions. The compressibility of softmax-attended language is intrinsic to the data rather than the analysis framework.

Abstract: Introduces "Reinforce to Learn, Elect to Reason" (RLER), a dual paradigm for video reasoning that separates learning to generate evidence from obtaining a reliable answer. During training, the method uses group-relative reinforcement learning and three novel task-driven rewards to enhance the model's ability to produce structured, machine-checkable evidence. Title: Graph-to-Frame RAG: Visual-Space Knowledge Fusion for Training-Free and Auditable Video Reasoning

Abstract: When video reasoning requires external knowledge, current systems often use retrieval augmentation to provide missing context. However, appending textual or multi-clip evidence can dilute attention and increase cognitive load. To address this, the authors introduce Graph-to-Frame RAG (G2F-RAG), a training-free and auditable method that delivers knowledge in the visual space. In the offline stage, a video knowledge graph is constructed integrating entities, events, spatial relations, and linked world knowledge. During the online stage, a hierarchical multi-agent controller determines if external knowledge is necessary, retrieves relevant information, and renders it as a reasoning frame attached to the video. This approach reduces cognitive load and provides an explicit, inspectable evidence trail. G2F-RAG demonstrates consistent performance gains across various benchmarks, particularly in knowledge-intensive scenarios.

**arXiv:2604.04359v1** (cs)

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems have been widely used in modern large language models (LLMs) because they enhance generation quality while minimizing the required input context length. This research focuses on RAG systems for long-document question answering. Existing methods heavily rely on LLM descriptions, leading to high resource usage and latency, repetitive content across different levels, and inaccuracies due to insufficient grounding in the source text. To address these issues, the authors propose GroundedKG-RAG, a RAG system where the knowledge graph is explicitly derived from and anchored in the source document. Nodes in GroundedKG represent entities and actions, while edges denote temporal or semantic relationships, all grounded in the original sentences. The knowledge graph is constructed using semantic role labeling (SRL) and abstract meaning representation (AMR) parsing, embedded for retrieval, and applied during querying to retrieve the most relevant sentences for answering questions. Evaluations on the NarrativeQA dataset show that GroundedKG-RAG performs comparably to a state-of-the-art proprietary long-context model but at lower costs and outperforms a competitive baseline. Additionally, the proposed GroundedKG is human-readable and facilitates result auditing and error analysis.

Spatially-Weighted CLIP (SW-CLIP) is proposed as a novel framework for street-view geo-localization. This method incorporates spatial autocorrelation into vision-language contrastive learning. Unlike traditional CLIP-based approaches, SW-CLIP uses Tobler's First Law of Geography to model geographic relationships using distance-aware soft supervision. It introduces a location-as-text representation to encode geographic positions and replaces one-hot InfoNCE targets with spatially weighted soft labels based on geodesic distance. Additionally, it employs a neighborhood-consistency regularization to maintain local spatial structure in the embedding space. Experiments show that SW-CLIP improves geo-localization accuracy, reduces long-tail errors, and enhances spatial coherence compared to standard CLIP.

Title: REAM: Merging Improves Pruning of Experts in LLMs

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models (LLMs) face significant memory challenges due to their size. Traditional methods like weight pruning and quantization are used to reduce memory usage. This paper introduces Router-weighted Expert Activation Merging (REAM), which merges experts instead of pruning them, thereby preserving performance better. REAM is evaluated against REAP and other methods using multiple-choice (MC) question answering and generative (GEN) benchmarks. Results indicate a trade-off between MC and GEN performance depending on calibration data mix. REAM often outperforms baseline methods and sometimes matches the performance of uncompressed models.

Title:Talk2AI: A Longitudinal Dataset of Human--AI Persuasive Conversations

Abstract:Talk2AI is a large-scale longitudinal dataset of 3,080 conversations (totaling 30,800 turns) between human participants and Large Language Models (LLMs). It aims to support research on persuasion, opinion change, and human-AI interaction. Abstract: The paper discusses the evolution of diverse complex agents under tight evaluation budgets using Large Language Model (LLM)-guided methods. It compares three optimization paradigms—Elo tournament selection (RoboPhD), Pareto-based selection (GEPA), and greedy hill-climbing (Autoresearch)—across four benchmarks. RoboPhD outperforms the other two methods on three out of four benchmarks, showcasing its effectiveness in evolving complex agents under limited evaluation resources.

Key Findings:
- RoboPhD introduces validation-free evolution, utilizing Elo competition on training data to simultaneously evaluate and evolve agents.
- The study demonstrates significant improvements in accuracy, evolving a 22-line seed agent into a 1,013-line multi-strategy system with an accuracy increase from 27.8% to 65.8%.

Title: Generative models for decision-making under distributional shift

Abstract: This paper discusses the use of modern generative models, such as flow- and score-based methods, as tools for constructing decision-relevant distributions in scenarios where the deployment distribution differs from the historical data distribution. **Abstract Summary:** The paper introduces "Soft Tournament Equilibrium" (STE), a framework for evaluating general-purpose artificial agents, especially those based on large language models. Traditional ranking methods are inadequate for assessing these agents due to the non-transitive nature of their interactions. STE proposes a set-valued core approach derived from classical tournament theory, offering a more stable and nuanced evaluation method through probabilistic modeling and differentiable operators for continuous tournament solutions.

**Key Points:**
- Addresses challenges in evaluating AI agents with non-transitive interactions.
- Introduces STE as a differentiable framework for learning set-valued tournament solutions.
- Provides theoretical foundations proving consistency with classical solutions.
- Specifies an experimental protocol for validation on synthetic and real-world benchmarks.

Abstract: Agent skills, which are reusable, domain-specific knowledge artifacts, have become a popular mechanism for extending LLM-based agents. However, formally benchmarking skill usage performance remains scarce. This study conducts the first comprehensive analysis of skill utility under progressively challenging realistic settings. https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04323v1
Effects of Generative AI Errors on User Reliance Across Task Difficulty

This paper investigates how users react to errors in generative AI outputs across different task difficulties. The authors conducted a 3x2 experiment (N=577) with error rates of 10%, 30%, or 50% on both easy and hard diagram generation tasks. They found that higher error rates reduced user reliance on the AI system. However, contrary to expectations, errors in easier tasks did not significantly decrease user reliance compared to those in harder tasks, indicating that users may not be particularly sensitive to the "jaggedness" of AI performance. The study suggests that future research should explore how varying task difficulty alongside other characteristics of AI errors affects user behavior.

Abstract:The increasing frequency and severity of climate-related disasters have intensified the need for real-time monitoring, early warning, and informed decision-making. Earth Observation (EO), powered by satellite data and Machine Learning (ML), offers powerful tools to address these challenges. Foundation Models (FMs) have transformed EO ML by enabling general-purpose pretraining on large-scale remote sensing datasets. However, most existing models depend on high-resolution satellite imagery with low revisit rates, which limits their effectiveness for rapidly changing phenomena and urgent emergency responses. This research introduces HighFM, an initial approach to creating a Foundation Model for handling high-temporal-resolution, multispectral Earth Observation data. Pretrained models are then fine-tuned on cloud masking and active fire detection tasks. The study benchmarks its SEVIRI pretrained Vision Transformers against traditional and recent geospatial FMs, showing consistent improvements in balanced accuracy and IoU metrics. Abstract: Personalized Large Language Model (LLM) systems have made significant advancements but often operate in environments where user preferences are stable and ground truths are either absent or subjective. 1. Behavioral memory complexity, where investor behaviors evolve over time, are self-contradictory, and have financial implications.

Abstract: Physiological foundation models (FMs) have shown promise for biosignal representation learning but are mostly limited to a single modality like EEG, ECG, or PPG due to the scarcity of paired multimodal datasets. Title: APPA: Adaptive Preference Pluralistic Alignment for Fair Federated RLHF of LLMs

Abstract: Aligning large language models (LLMs) with diverse human preferences requires pluralistic alignment, where a single model must respect the values of multiple distinct groups simultaneously. Authors: Aobo Chen, Chenxu Zhao, Chenglin Miao, Mengdi Huai

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and large reasoning models (LRMs) enhance data mining applications through strong semantic understanding and explicit multi-step reasoning traces. Continuous Acoustic Wave Networks (CAWN) for Autoregressive Language Modeling is introduced as a fully continuous sequence-mixing architecture. Unlike traditional Transformer models, CAWN uses a causal Phase Accumulation mechanism that operates in linear time relative to sequence length ($O(L)$). The paper highlights the introduction of a dual-gated Selective Phase Resonance mechanism to prevent signal degradation over long sequences, incorporating Frequency-Dependent Retention, Hard-Threshold Gating via Straight-Through Estimation, and a Temporal Syntax Cache. Additionally, the paper mentions replacing standard dense linear projections with Depth-wise Harmonic Convolutions and Block Attention Residuals for efficient state routing. The model was scaled to a 150M-parameter size and trained on a 100-billion-token corpus, demonstrating robust vocabulary acquisition and denoising capabilities.

Title:Combee: Scaling Prompt Learning for Self-Improving Language Model Agents

**arXiv:2604.04239v1**

This paper, titled "Good Rankings, Wrong Probabilities: A Calibration Audit of Multimodal Cancer Survival Models," examines the calibration of multimodal deep learning models used in predicting cancer survival. These models combine whole-slide histopathology images with genomic data. While these models show strong performance in terms of the concordance index, the study finds that the survival probabilities derived from these models are often poorly calibrated. Specifically, the research conducted the first systematic fold-level calibration audit of multimodal models, finding that most models failed calibration tests across multiple folds. Post-hoc Platt scaling was found to reduce miscalibration without affecting discrimination. The study concludes that the concordance index alone is insufficient for evaluating survival models intended for clinical use.

Title:Pedagogical Safety in Educational Reinforcement Learning: Formalizing and Detecting Reward Hacking in AI Tutoring Systems

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is increasingly used to personalize instruction in intelligent tutoring systems, yet lacks a formal framework for defining and evaluating pedagogical safety. The study introduces a four-layer model of pedagogical safety for educational RL, including structural, progress, behavioral, and alignment safety. It proposes the Reward Hacking Severity Index (RHSI) to measure misalignment between proxy rewards and genuine learning.

The framework was evaluated through a controlled simulation involving 120 sessions across four conditions and three learner profiles, totaling 18,000 interactions. Results indicate that an engagement-optimized agent favored high-engagement actions without direct mastery gains, leading to strong measured performance but limited learning progress. While a multi-objective reward formulation reduced this issue, it did not fully resolve it. A constrained architecture combining prerequisite enforcement and minimum cognitive demand significantly reduced reward hacking, lowering RHSI from 0.317 to 0.102.

These findings suggest that reward design alone may be insufficient to ensure pedagogically aligned behavior in educational RL. The paper emphasizes pedagogical safety as a critical research problem at the intersection of AI safety and intelligent educational systems.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI), Computers and Society (cs.CY), Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Title: TimeSeek: Temporal Reliability of Agentic Forecasters

Abstract: We introduce TimeSeek, a benchmark for studying how the reliability of agentic LLM forecasters changes over a prediction market's lifecycle. The study evaluates 10 frontier models on 150 CFTC-regulated Kalshi binary markets at five temporal checkpoints, totaling 15,000 forecasts. Results indicate that models perform best early in a market's life and on high-uncertainty markets, but less competitively near resolution and on strong-consensus markets. Web search enhances the pooled Brier Skill Score (BSS) for all models overall, though it negatively impacts performance in 12% of model-checkpoint pairs. Simple two-model ensembles can reduce errors without surpassing the market overall. This suggests a need for time-aware evaluation and selective-deference policies rather than relying solely on a single market snapshot or uniform tool-use settings.

Abstract: Reasoning Vision-Language Models (VLMs) may increase accuracy while losing visual grounding during reasoning. This leads to dangerous situations where predictions are confident but lack grounding, a failure mode undetectable by text-only monitoring. Evaluations on MathVista, HallusionBench, and MMMU_Pro reveal a widespread evidence-collapse phenomenon, where attention to annotated evidence regions significantly decreases. Adding vision features using a single global linear rule is unreliable and often degrades performance under cross-dataset transfer. An entropy-vision interaction model indicates that low-entropy, visually disengaged predictions pose risks in sustained visual-reference tasks but are harmless in symbolic tasks. The study supports the need for task-aware multimodal monitoring for safe deployment under distribution shifts.

Schema-Aware Planning and Hybrid Knowledge Toolset for Reliable Knowledge Graph Triple Verification

Abstract: Knowledge Graphs (KGs) are crucial for AI systems but can introduce noise during automated construction. Current triple verification methods often suffer from single-source bias and lack dynamic adaptability. To address these issues, researchers from Harbin Institute of Technology and collaborators propose SHARP (Schema-Hybrid Agent for Reliable Prediction). This autonomous agent uses a Memory-Augmented Mechanism combined with Schema-Aware Strategic Planning to enhance reasoning stability. Additionally, it employs an enhanced ReAct loop with a Hybrid Knowledge Toolset to integrate internal KG structure and external textual evidence for cross-verification. Experimental results on FB15K-237 and Wikidata5M-Ind datasets demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods, achieving accuracy gains of 4.2% and 12.9%, respectively. SHARP also offers transparent evidence chains for improved interpretability and robustness in complex verification tasks.

**arXiv:2604.04184v1** (cs)

Abstract: Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) have achieved strong performance in various video understanding tasks. However, existing systems are mostly offline and unsuitable for live video streams requiring continuous observation and immediate response. The proposed AURA (Always-On Understanding and Real-Time Assistance) is an end-to-end streaming visual interaction framework that enables a unified VideoLLM to continuously process video streams and support real-time question answering and proactive responses. AURA integrates context management, data construction, training objectives, and deployment optimization for stable long-term interaction. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on streaming benchmarks and supports a real-time demo system with ASR and TTS running at 2 FPS on two 80G accelerators. **arXiv:2604.04183v1**

Abstract: Extreme far-distance video person re-identification (ReID) faces challenges such as scale compression, resolution degradation, motion blur, and viewpoint mismatch. This work adapts large-scale vision-language models to improve performance under these conditions. Key improvements include upgrading the visual backbone from ViT-B/16 to ViT-L/14, introducing selective fine-tuning, and incorporating a lightweight temporal attention mechanism. The method also retains adapter-based and prompt-conditioned cross-view learning and refines retrieval through optimized k-reciprocal re-ranking. On the DetReIDX benchmark, the approach achieved mAP scores of 46.69 (A2G), 41.23 (G2A), and 22.98 (A2A), resulting in an overall mAP of 35.73.

**arXiv:2604.04182v1** (cs)

Abstract: Non-stationary environments require agents to revise previously learned action values when contingencies change. This study evaluates large language models (LLMs) such as DeepSeek-V3.2, Gemini-3, and GPT-5.2 in a probabilistic reversal-learning task. The findings reveal that LLMs exhibit rigid adaptation, showing weak loss sensitivity and persistent behavior after reversals. Hierarchical reinforcement-learning fits suggest that rigidity arises from weak loss learning, inflated policy determinism, or value polarization. These results highlight the need for reversal-sensitive diagnostics and volatility-aware models for assessing LLMs in non-stationary uncertainty.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly integrated into fact-checking pipelines, formal logic is often suggested as a method to reduce bias, errors, and hallucinations in their outputs. Subjects: Machine Learning (cs.LG)

Abstract: This paper proposes a framework for uncertainty-aware foundation modeling in clinical data. The framework represents each patient as a distribution over plausible latent states rather than a single point embedding. It learns set-valued representations and enforces consistency across partial views of the same patient, capturing invariantly inferable information while explicitly encoding epistemic uncertainty. The model integrates multimodal encoders and scalable self-supervised objectives, including reconstruction, contrastive alignment, and distributional regularization. The approach shows improved predictive performance, robustness under missing data, and better uncertainty calibration compared to existing strong baselines.

Title:Readable Minds: Emergent Theory-of-Mind-Like Behavior in LLM Poker Agents

Abstract: Theory of Mind (ToM) -- the ability to model others' mental states -- is fundamental to human social cognition. Autonomous LLM agents playing Texas Hold'em poker develop sophisticated opponent models when equipped with persistent memory. Memory is necessary and sufficient for ToM-like behavior emergence; agents with memory reach ToM Levels 3-5, while those without remain at Level 0. Strategic deception occurs exclusively in memory-equipped conditions. Domain expertise enhances ToM application but isn't necessary for emergence. Agents with ToM deviate from optimal play to exploit specific opponents, similar to expert human play. Mental models are expressed in natural language and readable.

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

**arXiv:2604.04155v1**

Abstract: Foundation models for biology and physics optimize predictive accuracy but fail to preserve the continuous geometry of modeled systems due to the Geometric Alignment Tax, which arises from forcing continuous manifolds through discrete categorical bottlenecks. Replacing cross-entropy with a continuous head reduces geometric distortion significantly. Evaluations reveal three failure regimes: Local-Global Decoupling, Representational Compression, and Geometric Vacuity. No model achieves simultaneous low distortion, high mutual information, and global coherence.

**arXiv:2604.04144v1**

Abstract: The goal of language model personalization is to tailor a single language model to each user's preferences. However, maintaining individual models for each user is impractical due to computational and memory constraints. This research introduces PALM (Portfolio of Aligned Language Models), an algorithm that selects a small set of language models to represent diverse user preferences efficiently. User preferences are modeled using a multi-dimensional weight vector, and the algorithm ensures that for any given preference vector, there exists a near-optimal language model in the selected portfolio. The study offers theoretical guarantees on the size and quality of the language model portfolio, highlighting the balance between system costs and personalization effectiveness. Empirical results confirm the theoretical findings, demonstrating increased output diversity compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Real-world exposure correction faces challenges due to spatially non-uniform degradations. **arXiv:2604.04131v1** (cs)

Subjects: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI)

Abstract: This paper introduces Profile-Then-Reason (PTR), a bounded execution framework designed for structured tool-augmented reasoning. In PTR, a language model first creates an explicit workflow, followed by deterministic or guarded operators executing this workflow. A verifier then evaluates the workflow's trace, and repair is initiated only if the original workflow becomes unreliable. The framework is mathematically formulated as a composition of profile, routing, execution, verification, repair, and reasoning operators. Under bounded repair conditions, the number of language-model calls is limited to two in normal cases and three in the worst-case scenario. Experimental results show that PTR outperforms a ReAct baseline in 16 out of 24 configurations across six benchmarks and four language models. The study indicates that PTR performs better on retrieval-centered and decomposition-heavy tasks compared to reactive execution methods.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04131v1
**arXiv:2604.04129v1** (cs)

\[Submitted on 5 Apr 2026\]

Title:Measuring Robustness of Speech Recognition from MEG Signals Under Distribution Shift

Abstract:This study evaluates the robustness of speech recognition from non-invasive magnetoencephalography (MEG) signals under distribution shifts. It compares various neural network architectures including residual convolutional neural networks (CNNs), an STFT-based CNN, and a CNN--Transformer hybrid. The research highlights that instance normalization significantly improves model performance, achieving a 60.95% F1-macro score on the test set. However, without instance normalization, models suffer from significant validation-to-test degradation, indicating that normalization-related distribution shifts are a major challenge for generalization. The MEGConformer model shows consistent performance across validation and test sets, suggesting that better handling of normalization-related distribution shifts is crucial for improving non-invasive phoneme decoding reliability.

Title: SARES-DEIM: Sparse Mixture-of-Experts Meets DETR for Robust SAR Ship Detection

Abstract: Ship detection in Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) imagery faces challenges due to coherent speckle noise, complex coastal clutter, and small-scale targets. The proposed method, SARES-DEIM, uses a domain-aware detection framework based on DETR. It includes SARESMoE, which employs a sparse gating mechanism to filter noise and maintain computational efficiency. Additionally, the Space-to-Depth Enhancement Pyramid (SDEP) improves the localization of small targets. Experiments show superior performance on benchmark datasets, achieving mAP50:95 of 76.4% and mAP50 of 93.8% on the HRSID dataset.

Title:Shorter, but Still Trustworthy? An Empirical Study of Chain-of-Thought Compression

Abstract:This study evaluates the impact of compressing chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning models on their trustworthiness. The research focuses on three dimensions: safety, hallucination resistance, and multilingual robustness. It finds that CoT compression often leads to decreased trustworthiness, with different methods showing varying degrees of degradation. The authors propose a normalized efficiency score to better compare different compression methods and introduce an alignment-aware DPO variant that reduces CoT length by 19.3% while maintaining trustworthiness.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04120v1
**arXiv:2604.04992v1**

Abstract: Safety-aligned Large Language Models (LLMs) undergo refusal training to reject harmful requests. However, the effectiveness of these mechanisms under emotionally charged stimuli remains unexplored. This study introduces FreakOut-LLM, a framework that investigates whether emotional context compromises safety alignment in adversarial settings. Using validated psychological stimuli, the research evaluates how emotional priming through system prompts affects jailbreak susceptibility across ten LLMs. Three conditions—stress, relaxation, and neutral—are tested using scenarios from established psychological protocols, alongside a no-prompt baseline. Attack success is evaluated using HarmBench on AdvBench prompts. Results indicate that stress priming increases jailbreak success by 65.2% compared to neutral conditions (z = 5.93, p < 0.001; OR = 1.67, Cohen's d = 0.28), whereas relaxation priming has no significant effect (p = 0.84). Five out of ten models exhibit significant vulnerability, particularly in open-weight models. Logistic regression on 59,800 queries confirms stress as the sole significant condition predictor after controlling for prompt length and model identity. Psychological state significantly predicts attack success (|r| ≥ 0.70 across five instruments; all p < 0.001 in individual-level logistic regression). These findings highlight emotional context as a measurable attack surface with implications for real-world AI deployment in high-stress environments.
https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2604.04992v1
Hypothesis Graph Refinement (HGR) is proposed as a framework for embodied navigation that improves upon existing graph-based navigation systems. The key components of HGR include:

1. Semantic hypothesis module: Estimates context-conditioned semantic distributions over frontiers and ranks exploration targets based on goal relevance, travel cost, and uncertainty.
2. Verification-driven cascade correction: Compares on-site observations against predicted semantics and retracts erroneous predictions along with their dependent nodes when mismatches occur.

Evaluation results show that HGR achieved a 72.41% success rate and 56.22% SPL on the GOAT-Bench dataset, demonstrating significant improvements over baseline methods. Additionally, diagnostic analysis revealed that cascade correction reduced revisits to erroneous regions by 4.5 times and accounted for 67% of corrected prediction errors.
